The sequence $(\psi _ N)$ is decreasing because $\varphi _ j\leq 0$, and $\| \psi _ N\| _{L^1}\leq \sup _ j\| \varphi _ j\| _{L^1}<\infty $ by proposition 14.2.1. By monotone convergence the limit $\psi $ is integrable, so it is not identically $-\infty $, and theorem 8.3.1, applied on coordinate balls after adding local potentials of $C\omega $, shows that $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,C\omega )$. On $K$ every term $2^{-j}\varphi _ j$ is at most $-1$, so $\psi =-\infty $ on $K$. On every coordinate ball $U$ with a smooth function $h$ such that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=C\omega $, the function $\psi +h$ is plurisubharmonic and equals $-\infty $ on $K\cap U$. Hence $K$ is pluripolar, a contradiction. The last assertion follows: bounds for $\sup _ K\varphi $ give bounds for $\sup _ X\varphi $, and proposition 14.2.1 applies. $\square$

Corollary 14.2.5. Let $(\varphi _ i)$ be a decreasing net in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ whose suprema $\sup _ X\varphi _ i$ are bounded below. Then its pointwise infimum $\varphi $ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $\varphi _ i\to \varphi $ in $L^1(X)$, and $\sup _ X\varphi _ i\to \sup _ X\varphi $.

Proof. Fix an index $i_0$. For $i\geq i_0$ the suprema are bounded above by $\sup _ X\varphi _{i_0}$, so the tail of the net lies in a compact subset of $L^1(X)$ by proposition 14.2.1, and a subnet converges in $L^1$ to some $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. For each fixed $i$, the members of the subnet are eventually below $\varphi _ i$, so $\psi \leq \varphi _ i$ almost everywhere, and therefore everywhere, as noted at the beginning of this section. Hence the pointwise infimum $\varphi $ is at least $\psi $ and is not identically $-\infty $. Applying theorem 8.3.1 on the coordinate balls of a finite cover, after adding local potentials of $\theta $, we find that $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $\varphi _ i\to \varphi $ in $L^1(X)$. The continuity of the supremum in proposition 14.2.1 gives the last assertion. $\square$

Definition 14.2.6. Let $\varphi ,\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. Recall from section 13.5 that $\varphi $ is more singular than $\psi $, written $\varphi \preceq \psi $, if $\varphi \leq \psi +C$ on $X$ for some constant $C$; we then also say that $\psi $ is less singular than $\varphi $. A potential $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ has minimal singularities if every $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ is more singular than $\psi $. If $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\ne \varnothing $, put

\[ V_\theta =\bigl (\sup \{ \varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta ):\varphi \leq 0\} \bigr )^*, \]

where ${}^*$ denotes the upper semicontinuous regularization.

Lemma 14.2.7. Suppose that $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\neq \varnothing $. Then $V_\theta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, $\sup _ XV_\theta =0$, and $\varphi \leq V_\theta +\sup _ X\varphi $ for every $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$; in particular, $V_\theta $ has minimal singularities. Any two potentials with minimal singularities differ by a bounded function on the set where both are finite, and the notion of minimal singularities depends only on the class $\{ \theta \} $.

Proof. The family whose upper envelope defines $V_\theta $ in definition 14.2.6 is nonempty, because $\varphi -\sup _ X\varphi $ belongs to it for every $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, and it is bounded above by $0$. Theorem 8.3.2, applied on coordinate balls after adding smooth local potentials of $\theta $, therefore gives $V_\theta \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and $V_\theta \leq 0$; and $\sup _ XV_\theta =0$, because $V_\theta \geq \varphi -\sup _ X\varphi $ for every $\varphi $. The same inequality reads $\varphi \leq V_\theta +\sup _ X\varphi $, so $V_\theta $ has minimal singularities. Any two potentials with minimal singularities are each more singular than the other, so their difference is bounded on the set where both are finite. Replacing $\theta $ by $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ with $h$ smooth replaces $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ by $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )-h$; since $h$ is bounded, the notion of minimal singularities depends only on the class $\{ \theta \} $. $\square$

Lelong numbers of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions are defined pointwise as in corollary 12.3.2: if $\varphi =v+g$ near $y$, with $v$ plurisubharmonic and $g$ smooth, then $\nu (\varphi ,y)=\nu (v,y)$, which does not depend on the decomposition. Hence the rules of proposition 12.1.4, the characterization of $\nu (\varphi ,y)$ as the largest $c\geq 0$ with $\varphi \leq c\log |z-z(y)|^2+O(1)$ near $y$ (proposition 12.1.1), and the upper semicontinuity (12.8) along sequences $\varphi _ j=v_ j-g$ with a common smooth $g$, all carry over.

Recall from proposition 12.3.5 the generic Lelong number of a plurisubharmonic function along an irreducible analytic subset, for instance along a prime divisor (an irreducible hypersurface). For a quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\varphi $ on a complex manifold and an irreducible analytic subset $Z$, we define in the same way the generic Lelong number $\nu (\varphi ,Z)=\inf _{y\in Z}\nu (\varphi ,y)$ of $\varphi $ along $Z$. The infimum is attained at every point of $Z$ outside a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $Z$. Indeed, $\lambda =\nu (\varphi ,Z)$ is finite, and for every integer $j\geq 1$ the set $A_ j=\{ y\in Z:\nu (\varphi ,y)\geq \lambda +1/j\} $ is an analytic subset of $Z$: near each point of $Z$ it is the intersection of $Z$ with the set $\{ \nu (v,\cdot )\geq \lambda +1/j\} $ for a local plurisubharmonic function $v=\varphi -g$ as above, which is analytic by theorem 12.3.4. It is a proper subset of $Z$ by the definition of $\lambda $, and $\nu (\varphi ,y)=\lambda $ at every point $y\in Z\setminus \bigcup _ jA_ j$. By proposition 12.3.5, the complement in $Z$ of a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $Z$ is dense in $Z$; in particular, $\nu (\varphi ,Z)$ is attained on a dense subset of $Z$. If $\pi \colon Y\to X$ is a proper bimeromorphic holomorphic map from a complex manifold $Y$ and $E\subset Y$ is a prime divisor, we put $\nu (\varphi ,E)=\nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,E)$ whenever $\varphi \circ \pi $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, as in proposition 12.3.5.

Proposition 14.2.8. Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be a proper bimeromorphic holomorphic map from a complex manifold $Y$. Then $Y$ is compact and connected, and $\varphi \mapsto \varphi \circ \pi $ is a bijection from $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ onto $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$, which is continuous for convergence in $L^1(X)$ and $L^1(Y)$, the latter taken with respect to any smooth positive volume form. For every prime divisor $E$ on $Y$, the generic Lelong numbers $\nu (\varphi ,E)=\nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,E)$ are bounded above uniformly for $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$.

Proof. The manifold $Y=\pi ^{-1}(X)$ is compact, because $\pi $ is proper. By the definition of bimeromorphic maps (section A.3), there is a nowhere dense analytic subset $E_0\subset X$ such that $\pi ^{-1}(E_0)$ is nowhere dense and $\pi $ restricts to an isomorphism of $Y\setminus \pi ^{-1}(E_0)$ onto $X\setminus E_0$. The set $X\setminus E_0$ is connected by lemma A.1.7, hence so is its dense preimage, and therefore $Y$ is connected. The image $\pi (Y)$ is closed, since proper maps are closed, and it contains the dense set $X\setminus E_0$; so $\pi $ is surjective. By proposition A.7.3 there is an analytic subset $A\subset X$ of codimension at least two such that $\pi $ is an isomorphism over $X\setminus A$. Then $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ is an analytic subset of $Y$ different from $Y$, because $\pi $ is surjective. Since $Y$ is connected, $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ is locally contained in the zero set of a holomorphic function that does not vanish identically on a connected neighborhood (section A.1), so it is nowhere dense and a null set (lemma 7.4.1). The same holds for $A$ in $X$.

If $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, let $B$ be a coordinate ball and $h$ a smooth function on $B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $ (lemma 13.1.2). On every connected coordinate neighborhood $W\subset \pi ^{-1}(B)$ the function $(\varphi +h)\circ \pi $ is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $, by proposition 8.2.3, and the second case is excluded because $\varphi +h$ is finite almost everywhere on the image under the biholomorphism $\pi $ of the nonempty open set $W\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$. Hence $\varphi \circ \pi =(\varphi +h)\circ \pi -h\circ \pi $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, with $\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi \circ \pi )=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((\varphi +h)\circ \pi )\geq 0$ on $\pi ^{-1}(B)$. Thus $\varphi \circ \pi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. If $\varphi \circ \pi =\varphi '\circ \pi $, then $\varphi =\varphi '$ on $X\setminus A$, hence almost everywhere, and therefore everywhere, as noted at the beginning of this section; this proves injectivity.

Conversely, let $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. Over $X\setminus A$ it descends to the function $\psi \circ \pi ^{-1}$, which is $\theta $-plurisubharmonic there. It is bounded above, because $\psi $ is upper semicontinuous on the compact manifold $Y$. On a coordinate ball $B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $ on $B$, the plurisubharmonic function $\psi \circ \pi ^{-1}+h$ on $B\setminus A$ is therefore locally bounded above near $A$, and it extends to a plurisubharmonic function on $B$ by theorem 8.4.1. The extensions on two balls agree outside the null set $A$, hence everywhere, so they define $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. The pullback $\varphi \circ \pi $ equals $\psi $ outside the null set $\pi ^{-1}(A)$, hence everywhere. This proves surjectivity.

For continuity, we use proposition 14.2.1 on $X$ and on $Y$; by its last assertion, its assertions for a fixed background hold on $Y$. Let $\varphi _ j\to \varphi $ in $L^1(X)$. By the continuity of the supremum, it suffices to treat normalized functions, $\sup _ X\varphi _ j=\sup _ X\varphi =0$, since subtracting the suprema preserves $L^1$ convergence. The pullbacks have the same suprema, because $\pi $ is surjective, so they are relatively compact in $L^1(Y)$, and every cluster value lies in $\mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$. On compact subsets of $Y\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$, a change of variables shows that every $L^1$ cluster value of $(\varphi _ j\circ \pi )$ coincides almost everywhere with $\varphi \circ \pi $; since $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ is a null set, every cluster value is $\varphi \circ \pi $. A relatively compact sequence in a metric space with a single cluster value converges to it; hence $\varphi _ j\circ \pi \to \varphi \circ \pi $ in $L^1(Y)$.

Finally, $\nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,E)$ is the infimum over $y\in E$ of the point Lelong numbers $\nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,y)$. Near $y$ choose a smooth $g$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g=\pi ^*\theta $, so that the functions $\varphi \circ \pi +g$ are plurisubharmonic. By (12.8), applied to them, each $\nu (\varphi \circ \pi ,y)$ is upper semicontinuous for $L^1$ convergence of $\varphi \circ \pi $, hence, by the continuity just proved, for $L^1$ convergence of $\varphi $; and an infimum of upper semicontinuous functions is upper semicontinuous. Since Lelong numbers do not change when a constant is added, we may restrict to the normalized functions, which form a compact set by proposition 14.2.1. An upper semicontinuous function with finite values attains its maximum on a compact set, which gives the uniform bound. $\square$

Corollary 14.2.9. Let $B'$ be a coordinate ball in $X$ and $B\Subset B'$ a ball in the same coordinates, and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ be bounded near $\overline{B'}$. There is $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with $\psi \geq \varphi $, $\psi =\varphi $ on $X\setminus B$, $\psi $ bounded near $\overline B$, and $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n=0$ on $B$. It is the largest function in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ with these properties, and any two functions with these properties that are continuous on $\overline B$ coincide.

Proof. Choose a ball $B''$ in the same coordinates with $\overline B\subset B''$ and $\overline{B''}\subset B'$. Write $\theta =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h$ on $B''$, with $h$ smooth (lemma 13.1.2), and apply corollary 9.6.4 to the locally bounded plurisubharmonic function $\varphi +h$ on $B''$. Subtracting $h$ from the result on $B''$ and keeping $\varphi $ outside $B''$ gives $\psi $, since the result equals $\varphi +h$ on $B''\setminus B$; here $(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi )^ n=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\psi +h))^ n$ is the Bedford–Taylor product of the bounded plurisubharmonic function $\psi +h$. If $\psi '$ is another function with the stated properties, then by upper semicontinuity $\varlimsup _{z\to \zeta ,\, z\in B}\psi '(z)\leq \psi '(\zeta )=\varphi (\zeta )$ for every $\zeta \in \partial B$. Hence $\psi '+h$ belongs to the family whose upper envelope is $\psi +h$ in corollary 9.6.4, and $\psi '\leq \psi $ on $B$. If $\psi '$ is continuous on $\overline B$, then its boundary values $\varphi |_{\partial B}$ are continuous, and $\psi '+h$ is the solution of the homogeneous Dirichlet problem with the continuous boundary values $(\varphi +h)|_{\partial B}$; by the uniqueness in theorem 9.6.3, any two such continuous functions coincide. $\square$

14.3 Positivity cones and divisorial decomposition

Let $\alpha \in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ and fix a smooth representative $\theta \in \alpha $. By theorem 14.1.7, the closed currents in $\alpha $ are exactly the currents $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ with $\varphi $ a real distribution, so positivity properties of $\alpha $ become inequalities for $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $. Besides the positivity cones and the mixed Hodge index theorem, we prove Siu's decomposition of a closed positive current (theorem 14.3.7), which leads to modified nef classes.

Definition 14.3.1. A class $\alpha \in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ is

  • pseudo-effective if it contains a closed positive current;

  • Kähler if it contains a Kähler form;

  • nef if for every $\varepsilon >0$ it contains a smooth form $\theta _\varepsilon $ with $\theta _\varepsilon \geq -\varepsilon \omega $;

  • big if it contains a closed current $T$ with $T\geq \delta \omega $ for some $\delta >0$.

A closed real $(1,1)$-current $T$ with $T\geq \delta \omega $ for some $\delta >0$ is called a Kähler current. The cones of pseudo-effective, Kähler, nef and big classes are denoted $\mathcal E$, $\mathcal K$, $\mathcal N$ and $\mathcal B$. The zero class belongs to the pseudo-effective cone and to the nef cone.

These notions do not depend on $\omega $, because any two Hermitian forms on the compact manifold $X$ are bounded by constant multiples of each other (lemma 13.1.1). By corollary 14.1.9, $\alpha $ is pseudo-effective if and only if $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )\ne \varnothing $.

Proposition 14.3.2. The pseudo-effective cone $\mathcal E$ is closed and convex, and the Kähler cone $\mathcal K$ is open and convex. Moreover $\mathcal N=\overline{\mathcal K}\subset \mathcal E$, the Kähler cone $\mathcal K$ is the interior of $\mathcal N$, and the big cone $\mathcal B$ is the interior of $\mathcal E$.

Proof. Sums and positive multiples of positive currents, Kähler forms or nef representatives are of the same kind, which proves convexity of all four cones.

Let $\alpha _ j\to \alpha $ be pseudo-effective classes, with positive currents $T_ j\in \alpha _ j$. Since the $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of a distribution pairs to zero with the closed form $\omega ^{n-1}$, the trace masses are

\[ \int _ XT_ j\wedge \omega ^{n-1} =\int _ X\theta _{\alpha _ j}\wedge \omega ^{n-1}, \]

where $\theta _{\alpha _ j}$ are the representatives of (14.16); they are bounded because the classes converge. Let $K$ be a compact subset of a chart, $\chi $ a test function on the chart with $0\leq \chi \leq 1$ and $\chi =1$ on $K$, and $c'>0$ such that $\beta \leq c'\omega $ on $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $ (lemma 13.1.1), where $\beta $ is the Euclidean form (7.4) of the chart. By (14.12), applied with $\gamma _ j=c'\omega $ and $\gamma '_ j=\beta $, the form $(c'\omega )^{n-1}-\beta ^{n-1}$ is a sum of products of semipositive $(1,1)$-forms on $\operatorname{Supp}\chi $, hence strongly positive (proposition 7.2.1). Therefore $c'^{n-1}\omega ^{n-1}-\chi \beta ^{n-1}=(1-\chi )c'^{n-1}\omega ^{n-1}+\chi \bigl (c'^{n-1}\omega ^{n-1}-\beta ^{n-1}\bigr )$, with $\chi \beta ^{n-1}$ extended by zero outside the chart, is a smooth strongly positive form on $X$, so the trace measures satisfy $\mu _{T_ j}(K)\leq T_ j(\chi \beta ^{n-1})\leq c'^{n-1}\int _ XT_ j\wedge \omega ^{n-1}$, which is bounded independently of $j$. By the compactness assertion of theorem 7.2.5, a subsequence of $(T_ j)$ converges weakly to a closed positive current $T$, and $\{ T\} =\alpha $ because the class map is weakly continuous. Hence $\mathcal E$ is closed.

If $\omega _0\in \alpha $ is a Kähler form, then $\omega _0\geq c\omega $ for some $c>0$, and by (14.16) the form $\omega _0+\theta _{\alpha '-\alpha }\in \alpha '$ is Kähler whenever $C\| \alpha '-\alpha \| <c$; hence $\mathcal K$ is open. If $\alpha $ is nef and $\varepsilon >0$, a representative $\theta _\varepsilon \geq -\varepsilon \omega $ gives the Kähler form $\theta _\varepsilon +2\varepsilon \omega \in \alpha +2\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $, so $\alpha \in \overline{\mathcal K}$. Conversely, let Kähler classes $\alpha _ j$ with Kähler forms $\omega _ j\in \alpha _ j$ converge to $\alpha $. Then $\omega _ j+\theta _{\alpha -\alpha _ j}\in \alpha $ is at least $-C\| \alpha -\alpha _ j\| \omega $, so $\alpha $ is nef; thus $\mathcal N=\overline{\mathcal K}$, and $\mathcal N\subset \mathcal E$ because $\mathcal K\subset \mathcal E$ and $\mathcal E$ is closed.

Since $\mathcal K$ is open and contained in $\mathcal N$, it lies in the interior of $\mathcal N$. If $\alpha $ is nef and $t>0$, then $\alpha +t\{ \omega \} $ is Kähler: take a representative $\theta _\varepsilon \geq -\varepsilon \omega $ with $\varepsilon <t$. If $\alpha $ lies in the interior of $\mathcal N$, then $\alpha -t\{ \omega \} $ is nef for small $t>0$, and therefore $\alpha =(\alpha -t\{ \omega \} )+t\{ \omega \} $ is Kähler. Thus $\mathcal K$ is the interior of $\mathcal N$.

If $T\in \alpha $ and $T\geq \delta \omega $, then $T+\theta _{\alpha '-\alpha }\in \alpha '$ is positive whenever $C\| \alpha '-\alpha \| \leq \delta $, so a neighborhood of $\alpha $ lies in $\mathcal E$. Conversely, if $\alpha $ lies in the interior of $\mathcal E$, then $\alpha -\delta \{ \omega \} \in \mathcal E$ for some $\delta >0$, and adding $\delta \omega $ to a positive current in $\alpha -\delta \{ \omega \} $ gives a Kähler current in $\alpha $. Hence $\mathcal B$ is the interior of $\mathcal E$. $\square$

If $\theta _1,\ldots ,\theta _ n$ are smooth closed real $(1,1)$-forms, Stokes' formula shows that $\int _ X\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _ n$ is unchanged when one $\theta _ i$ is replaced by $\theta _ i+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ with $f$ smooth. We therefore write $\int _ X\alpha _1\cdots \alpha _ n=\int _ X\theta _1\wedge \cdots \wedge \theta _ n$ for the intersection number of classes $\alpha _ i=\{ \theta _ i\} $, and use the usual notation for powers, such as $\int _ X\alpha ^ n$. In the mixed Hodge index theorem below, the mixed scalar equation of lemma 14.1.4 turns a cohomological orthogonality condition into a pointwise one, to which the signature lemma for Hermitian matrices applies.

Theorem 14.3.3 (The mixed Hodge index theorem). Let $n\geq 2$, let $\omega _3,\ldots ,\omega _ n$ be Kähler forms on $X$, and put $\Omega =\omega _3\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _ n$, with $\Omega =1$ when $n=2$. The symmetric bilinear form

\[ Q(\alpha ,\beta )=\int _ Xa\wedge b\wedge \Omega , \qquad a\in \alpha ,\quad b\in \beta , \]

on $H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ has signature $(1,\dim H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)-1)$. For every Kähler class $\gamma $, it is negative definite on the hyperplane $\{ \alpha :Q(\alpha ,\gamma )=0\} $. For Kähler classes $\alpha ,\beta $,

14.18
\begin{equation} Q(\alpha ,\beta )^2\geq Q(\alpha ,\alpha )Q(\beta ,\beta ). \end{equation}

Proof. Let $\gamma $ be a Kähler class with a Kähler form $h\in \gamma $, and let $\alpha $ satisfy $Q(\alpha ,\gamma )=0$. Choose a smooth closed real form $a\in \alpha $; then $\int _ Xa\wedge h\wedge \Omega =0$. Apply lemma 14.1.4 with $A=h\wedge \Omega $, a product of $n-1$ Kähler forms, and $\eta =-a\wedge h\wedge \Omega $. The resulting $f$ makes $a'=a+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f\in \alpha $ satisfy $a'\wedge h\wedge \Omega =0$ at every point. At a point, in holomorphic coordinates, the wedge products of real $(1,1)$-forms are expressed by mixed discriminants of their Hermitian matrices, so lemma 7.2.3 gives $a'\wedge a'\wedge \Omega \leq 0$, with equality only if $a'$ vanishes at that point. By Stokes' formula,

\[ Q(\alpha ,\alpha )=\int _ Xa'\wedge a'\wedge \Omega \leq 0. \]

If this integral vanishes, the smooth nonpositive density $a'\wedge a'\wedge \Omega $ vanishes everywhere, so $a'=0$ and $\alpha =0$. Thus $Q$ is negative definite on the hyperplane $\{ Q(\cdot ,\gamma )=0\} $.

Since $Q(\gamma ,\gamma )=\int _ Xh^2\wedge \Omega >0$, every class splits uniquely as a multiple of $\gamma $ plus a class in that hyperplane. The space $H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ is finite dimensional by theorem 14.1.7, and $Q$ is positive on the line spanned by $\gamma $ and negative definite on its $Q$-orthogonal complement; this gives the signature. Finally, let $\alpha ,\beta $ be Kähler classes and write $\beta =c\alpha +\delta $ with $Q(\delta ,\alpha )=0$. Negative definiteness gives $Q(\beta ,\beta )=c^2Q(\alpha ,\alpha )+Q(\delta ,\delta )\leq c^2Q(\alpha ,\alpha )$, while $Q(\alpha ,\beta )=cQ(\alpha ,\alpha )$. Since $Q(\alpha ,\alpha )>0$, we obtain $Q(\alpha ,\beta )^2=c^2Q(\alpha ,\alpha )^2\geq Q(\alpha ,\alpha )Q(\beta ,\beta )$. $\square$

Corollary 14.3.4. Let $r\geq 1$, let $\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ r$ be nef classes, and let $k_1,\ldots ,k_ r\geq 0$ be integers with $\sum _ i k_ i=n$. Then

\[ \int _ X\alpha _1^{k_1}\cdots \alpha _ r^{k_ r} \geq \prod _{i:k_ i>0}\left(\int _ X\alpha _ i^ n\right)^{k_ i/n}. \]

In particular, for nef classes $\alpha _1,\ldots ,\alpha _ n$,

\[ \int _ X\alpha _1\cdots \alpha _ n \geq \prod _{i=1}^ n\left(\int _ X\alpha _ i^ n\right)^{1/n}. \]

Proof. Assume first that all $\alpha _ i$ are Kähler. Then all the intersection numbers $\int _ X\prod _ i\alpha _ i^{k_ i}$ are positive, and we put $b(k)=\log \int _ X\prod _ i\alpha _ i^{k_ i}$ for $k=(k_1,\ldots ,k_ r)$ with $\sum _ ik_ i=n$. If $n=1$, only one $k_ i$ is positive and there is nothing to prove; let $n\geq 2$. Let $i\neq j$ with $k_ i,k_ j\geq 1$, let $\Omega $ be the wedge product of Kähler representatives of the remaining $n-2$ factors, counted with multiplicity, and let $e_ i$ be the $i$th coordinate vector. Then (14.18) for $\alpha _ i,\alpha _ j$ reads

\[ 2b(k)\geq b(k+e_ i-e_ j)+b(k-e_ i+e_ j). \]

Fix the coordinates other than $i,j$ and put $s=k_ i+k_ j$. The inequality says that the finite sequence $l\mapsto b(\ldots ,l,\ldots ,s-l,\ldots )$, with $l$ in the $i$th and $s-l$ in the $j$th place, $0\leq l\leq s$, is concave: its consecutive differences decrease. A concave sequence lies above the linear interpolation between its endpoints, since the average of its first $l$ differences is at least the average of all $s$ differences. Consequently

\[ b(k)\geq \frac{k_ i}{s}b(k^{(i)})+\frac{k_ j}{s}b(k^{(j)}), \]

where $k^{(i)}$ is obtained from $k$ by putting all $s$ factors in the $i$th place and $k^{(j)}$ by putting them in the $j$th place.

We now prove $b(k)\geq \sum _ i(k_ i/n)\log \int _ X\alpha _ i^ n$ by induction on the number of positive coordinates of $k$. If only one coordinate is positive, this is an equality. Otherwise choose $i\ne j$ with $k_ i,k_ j\geq 1$. The vectors $k^{(i)}$ and $k^{(j)}$ have one positive coordinate fewer, and inserting the induction hypothesis for them into the last display gives the claim: for $m\ne i,j$ the coefficient of $\log \int _ X\alpha _ m^ n$ is $(k_ i/s+k_ j/s)\, k_ m/n=k_ m/n$, and the coefficients of $\log \int _ X\alpha _ i^ n$ and $\log \int _ X\alpha _ j^ n$ are $(k_ i/s)(s/n)=k_ i/n$ and $k_ j/n$. Exponentiating proves the corollary for Kähler classes.

For nef classes, $\alpha _ i+\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ is Kähler for every $\varepsilon >0$ by proposition 14.3.2, so the inequality holds for these classes. Each intersection number involved is a polynomial in $\varepsilon $ and tends to the corresponding number for the $\alpha _ i$ as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$; in particular the limits $\int _ X\alpha _ i^ n$ are nonnegative. Since $t\mapsto t^{k_ i/n}$ is continuous on $[0,\infty )$, the inequality passes to the limit. $\square$

The next proposition uses the cutoff-sum approximations $G_ m$ of lemma 13.4.1: for $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$ and integers $m\geq 1$, $G_ m$ has gentle analytic singularities (definition 13.1.11) of type $(m^{-1},\mathcal I(m\varphi ))$ and satisfies $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq -(c/m)\omega $, where $c>0$ (the constant $c'$ of that lemma) depends only on $(X,\omega )$ and not on $\theta $, $\varphi $ or $m$. In particular $G_ m$ is smooth near every point where $\mathcal I(m\varphi )$ is the unit ideal.

Proposition 14.3.5. A class that contains a closed positive current with locally bounded potentials is nef. A class that contains a Kähler current with locally bounded potentials is Kähler. Every big class contains a Kähler current with gentle analytic singularities.

Proof. Write the given current as $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ with $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. If $\varphi $ is bounded, then every $\mathcal I(m\varphi )$ is the unit ideal, so the approximations $G_ m$ are smooth and satisfy $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq -(c/m)\omega $; hence the class is nef. If moreover $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \omega $, then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta -\delta \omega )$, and the approximations for the background $\theta -\delta \omega $ satisfy $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq (\delta -c/m)\omega $; for large $m$ the smooth form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m$ is a Kähler form in the class. For the last assertion, let $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \omega $ be a Kähler current in a big class, with $\varphi $ not necessarily bounded. The same inequality shows that $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m$ is a Kähler current for large $m$, and $G_ m$ has gentle analytic singularities of type $(m^{-1},\mathcal I(m\varphi ))$ by lemma 13.4.1. $\square$

Corollary 14.3.6. If a closed positive current with minimal singularities is a Kähler current, then its class is Kähler. Moreover, in a Kähler class every potential with minimal singularities is bounded.

Proof. Write the current as $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \omega $, where $\varphi $ has minimal singularities, and normalize $\varphi \leq 0$. Choose $C'\geq 0$ with $-\theta \leq C'\omega $ (lemma 13.1.1). For $0<t\leq \delta /(\delta +C')$ the current

\[ (1-t)T+t\theta =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((1-t)\varphi \bigr )\geq \bigl ((1-t)\delta -tC'\bigr )\omega \geq 0 \]

is positive, so $(1-t)\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$. By minimality, $(1-t)\varphi \leq \varphi +C$ for a constant $C$, so $\varphi \geq -C/t$ at every point where $\varphi $ is finite, hence almost everywhere. Since $\varphi $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, the lower bound holds everywhere, as noted at the beginning of section 14.2. Thus $\varphi $ is bounded, and proposition 14.3.5 shows that the class is Kähler. For the second assertion, suppose that the class contains a Kähler form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. Then $u$ is smooth and $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, so a potential $\varphi $ with minimal singularities satisfies $u\leq \varphi +C$; hence $\varphi $ is bounded below, and it is bounded above as every quasi-plurisubharmonic function on $X$. $\square$

Recall from definition 12.1.2 that the Lelong number $\nu (T,x)$ of a closed positive $(1,1)$-current $T$ at $x\in X$ is the Lelong number at $x$ of any local plurisubharmonic potential of $T$. If $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ with $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, then $\varphi +h$ is such a potential wherever $h$ is smooth with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $, so $\nu (T,x)=\nu (\varphi ,x)$ in the sense recalled before proposition 14.2.8; in particular, these numbers do not depend on the background $\theta $. For a prime divisor $D\subset X$, the generic Lelong number of $T$ along $D$ (proposition 12.3.5) is therefore

\[ \nu (T,D)=\inf _{x\in D}\nu (T,x)=\inf _{x\in D}\nu (\varphi ,x)=\nu (\varphi ,D), \]

and by that proposition the infimum is attained at every point of $D$ outside a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $D$. We write $[D]$ for the current of integration along $D$ (lemma A.2.16); its coefficient measures are given by integration over $D_{\mathrm{reg}}$ and give no mass to $D_{\mathrm{sing}}$.

Theorem 14.3.7 (Siu's divisorial decomposition). Let $T$ be a closed positive $(1,1)$-current on $X$. Then $\nu (T,D)>0$ for at most countably many prime divisors $D$, the series below converges weakly, and

14.19
\begin{equation} T=R+\sum _ D\nu (T,D)[D], \end{equation}

where $R$ is a closed positive current with $\nu (R,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$. In particular $T-\nu (T,D)[D]\geq 0$ for every $D$. The decomposition is unique: if $T=R'+\sum _ Db_ D[D]$ for a weakly convergent series with coefficients $b_ D\geq 0$, at most countably many of them nonzero, and a closed positive current $R'$ with $\nu (R',D)=0$ for every $D$, then $b_ D=\nu (T,D)$ for every $D$ and $R'=R$.

Proof. We first subtract one divisor, then show that only countably many divisors occur and that the series converges, and finally identify the residual current and prove uniqueness.

Step 1: one divisor. Let $D$ be a prime divisor and $c=\nu (T,D)$; we show that $T-c[D]\geq 0$. Near a regular point of $D$ choose coordinates with $D=\{ z_1=0\} $ on a coordinate polydisc $W$ with $|z_1|<1$, and a plurisubharmonic potential $u$ of $T$ on $W$. Since the Lelong numbers of $u$ at the points of $D\cap W$ are at least $c$, proposition 12.3.6 gives $u\leq c\log |z_1|^2+C$ near the point. Off $D$ the function $u-c\log |z_1|^2$ is plurisubharmonic, and it is bounded above near $D$; by theorem 8.4.1 it extends to a plurisubharmonic function $v$. Since $v=u-c\log |z_1|^2$ almost everywhere and $[D]=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |z_1|^2$ near the point (theorem 7.4.5), we get $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v=T-c[D]$ there. Hence $T-c[D]$ is positive on a neighborhood of $D_{\mathrm{reg}}$, and it equals $T$ on $X\setminus D$; so it is positive on the open set $X\setminus D_{\mathrm{sing}}$.

To extend the positivity across $D_{\mathrm{sing}}$, let $\phi $ be a strongly positive test form of bidegree $(n-1,n-1)$. Since $T$ and $[D]$ have measure coefficients (theorem 7.2.5), there is a real measure $m_\phi $ on $X$, a combination of the coefficient measures with the coefficients of $\phi $ as densities, such that $(T-c[D])(\chi \phi )=\int \chi \, \mathrm dm_\phi $ for test functions $\chi $. The restriction of $m_\phi $ to the open set $X\setminus D_{\mathrm{sing}}$ is a positive measure, because $(T-c[D])(\chi \phi )\geq 0$ for test functions $\chi \geq 0$ supported there; and its restriction to $D_{\mathrm{sing}}$ is that of the positive measure $\chi \mapsto T(\chi \phi )$, because $[D]$ gives no mass to $D_{\mathrm{sing}}$. Hence $(T-c[D])(\phi )=m_\phi (X)\geq 0$, that is, $T-c[D]\geq 0$.

Step 2: the series. By theorem 12.3.4, each set $E_ j=\{ x:\nu (T,x)\geq 1/j\} $ is an analytic subset of $X$ different from $X$. Being compact, $E_ j$ has finitely many irreducible components $Z$ by corollary A.2.13. We first show:

14.20
\begin{equation} \begin{gathered} \text{a prime divisor $D$ contained in an analytic subset $E\neq X$}\\ \text{is an irreducible component of $E$.} \end{gathered} \end{equation}

An analytic subset of the connected manifold $X$ with an interior point is all of $X$, since the set of its interior points is open and, by the identity theorem applied to local defining functions, closed. Hence $E$ has no interior points, so its germs, and their branches, have dimension at most $n-1$, and $E_{\mathrm{sing}}$ has dimension at most $n-2$ at every point (proposition A.2.7, corollary A.2.10). At a point $y\in D_{\mathrm{reg}}$ the germ of $D$ has dimension $n-1$, so by monotonicity of dimension (see proposition A.2.7 and the remarks following it) it is not contained in the germ of $E_{\mathrm{sing}}$; thus there are points $y'\in D_{\mathrm{reg}}\cap E_{\mathrm{reg}}$. Near such a point $E$ is a complex submanifold of dimension at most $n-1$ containing the $(n-1)$-dimensional submanifold $D$, so $D$ and $E$ coincide near $y'$.

Let $F$ be the connected component of $E_{\mathrm{reg}}$ containing $y'$. The analytic subset $D\cap F$ of the connected manifold $F$ has an interior point, so it is all of $F$, and $D$ contains the closure $Z$ of $F$, an irreducible component of $E$ (corollary A.2.13). Since $D$ is compact and the components of $E$ form a locally finite family, $D$ meets only finitely many of them and is the union of its intersections with these. By induction on the number of sets, an irreducible set is not a finite union of analytic subsets different from itself; hence $D=D\cap Z'$ for a component $Z'$. Then $Z\subset D\subset Z'$, and since no component is contained in another, $D=Z$. This proves (14.20). In particular, two distinct prime divisors are not contained in one another, and every prime divisor $D$ with $\nu (T,D)\geq 1/j$, being contained in $E_ j$, is one of its finitely many components. Therefore at most countably many prime divisors have $\nu (T,D)>0$. Let $D_1,D_2,\ldots $ be an enumeration of them and put $S_ N=\sum _{k\leq N}\nu (T,D_ k)[D_ k]$.

We claim that $T-S_ N\geq 0$ for every $N$, and argue by induction; suppose that $T-S_{N-1}\geq 0$. Choose a point $x\in D_ N$ outside the proper analytic subsets $D_ N\cap D_ k$, $k<N$, and outside the exceptional sets of proposition 12.3.5 for $T$ and $T-S_{N-1}$ along $D_ N$. Such points exist, because by that proposition the complement in $D_ N$ of a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $D_ N$ is dense in $D_ N$. The currents $[D_ k]$, $k<N$, vanish near $x$, so $T-S_{N-1}=T$ near $x$, and therefore $\nu (T-S_{N-1},D_ N)=\nu (T-S_{N-1},x)=\nu (T,x)=\nu (T,D_ N)$. Step 1, applied to $T-S_{N-1}$, gives $T-S_ N\geq 0$. Consequently the trace measures of the $S_ N$ in every chart are bounded by that of $T$, and the masses $\int _ XS_ N\wedge \omega ^{n-1}$ are bounded by $\int _ XT\wedge \omega ^{n-1}$.

We now show that the sequence $(S_ N)$ converges weakly. Every test form is a finite linear combination of test forms $g\gamma $, where $g\geq 0$ is a test function supported in a chart and $\gamma $ is a strongly positive form with constant coefficients in that chart. To see this, use a partition of unity (proposition 7.1.3), the fact that strongly positive forms span the real forms (proposition 7.2.1), and write a real test function $g$ as $(g+M\psi )-M\psi $ with $\psi \geq 0$ a test function equal to one on $\operatorname{Supp}g$ and $M\geq \sup |g|$. For such $g\gamma $ the numbers $S_ N(g\gamma )$ increase with $N$, since $S_{N+1}-S_ N\geq 0$, and they are bounded by $T(g\gamma )$; hence they converge, and so does $S_ N(\phi )$ for every test form $\phi $. By the compactness assertion of theorem 7.2.5, a subsequence converges weakly to a closed positive current $S$, so $S_ N(\phi )\to S(\phi )$ for every $\phi $. Finally $R=T-S$ is closed and positive, as a weak limit of the closed positive currents $T-S_ N$.

Step 3: properties of $R$ and uniqueness. Fix a prime divisor $D$ and write $T=R+\nu (T,D)[D]+S_ D$, where $S_ D$ is the sum of the terms of the series for the other divisors. Lelong numbers are additive and nonnegative (proposition 12.1.4), and $\nu ([D],x)=\nu (\log |f|^2,x)=1$ at regular points $x$ of $D$, where $f$ is a local coordinate defining $D$. Hence, at a point of $D_{\mathrm{reg}}$ outside the countable union of proper analytic subsets where one of the currents $T$, $R$, $S_ D$ has a nongeneric Lelong number (such points exist by proposition 12.3.5, since $D_{\mathrm{sing}}$ is also a proper analytic subset of $D$),

\[ \nu (T,D)=\nu (R,D)+\nu (T,D)+\nu (S_ D,D)\geq \nu (R,D)+\nu (T,D). \]

Hence $\nu (R,D)=0$.

Now consider any decomposition $T=R'+\sum _ Db_ D[D]$ as in the statement, fix $D$, and let $S'_ D$ be the sum of the terms for the other divisors. We first show that the coefficient measures of $S'_ D$ give no mass to $D$. If $D'\neq D$ is another prime divisor, then $D\cap D'_{\mathrm{reg}}$ is an analytic subset of the connected manifold $D'_{\mathrm{reg}}$ (corollary A.2.13) which is not all of it, since otherwise $D'\subset D$, and then $D'=D$ by (14.20). Hence $D\cap D'_{\mathrm{reg}}$ is a null set for the surface measure of $D'_{\mathrm{reg}}$ (lemma 7.4.1, in local coordinates of $D'_{\mathrm{reg}}$), and $[D']$ gives no mass to $D$. Now let $K\subset D$ be a compact subset of a chart, and for large $m$ let $\chi _ m$ be test functions with $0\leq \chi _ m\leq 1$, equal to one on $K$ and supported in the $1/m$-neighborhood of $K$ (lemma 1.4.1), all supported in a fixed compact subset of the chart, on which a test function $\psi $ equals one. For the trace measures in the chart, the weak convergence of the series gives $\mu _{S'_ D}(\chi _ m)=\sum _{D'}b_{D'}\mu _{[D']}(\chi _ m)$. As $m\to \infty $, $\chi _ m\to \mathbf1_ K$ pointwise, and dominated convergence, applied to the measure $\mu _{S'_ D}$ and to the series, whose terms are bounded by $b_{D'}\mu _{[D']}(\psi )$ with $\sum _{D'}b_{D'}\mu _{[D']}(\psi )=\mu _{S'_ D}(\psi )<\infty $, gives $\mu _{S'_ D}(K)=\sum _{D'}b_{D'}\mu _{[D']}(K)=0$. Since $D$ is covered by countably many such compact sets, the trace measures of $S'_ D$, and hence its coefficient measures (theorem 7.2.5), vanish on $D$.

By Step 1, $S'_ D-\nu (S'_ D,D)[D]\geq 0$; comparing the trace measures of both sides on $D$, where $[D]$ has positive trace mass, forces $\nu (S'_ D,D)=0$. Additivity of Lelong numbers at generic points of $D$, applied to $T=R'+b_ D[D]+S'_ D$, gives $\nu (T,D)=0+b_ D+0$. Thus $b_ D=\nu (T,D)$ for every $D$, and $R'=R$. $\square$

Definition 14.3.8. A class $\alpha $ is modified nef if for every $\varepsilon >0$ there is a closed current $T_\varepsilon \in \alpha $ with $T_\varepsilon \geq -\varepsilon \omega $ and $\nu (T_\varepsilon +\varepsilon \omega ,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$. We write $\mathcal{MN}$ for the cone of modified nef classes. A closed positive $(1,1)$-current $T$ with $\nu (T,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$ is called non-divisorial.

The set $\mathcal{MN}$ is a convex cone. Indeed, multiplying $T_\varepsilon $ by $t>0$ shows that $t\alpha $ is modified nef, with $tT_{\varepsilon /t}$ for the allowance $\varepsilon $. If $\alpha ,\alpha '$ are modified nef, with currents $T_\varepsilon ,T'_\varepsilon $, then $T_{\varepsilon /2}+T'_{\varepsilon /2}\geq -\varepsilon \omega $ lies in $\alpha +\alpha '$. At a point of a prime divisor $D$ at which the Lelong numbers of the positive currents $T_{\varepsilon /2}+\varepsilon \omega /2$ and $T'_{\varepsilon /2}+\varepsilon \omega /2$ attain their generic values $0$ (proposition 12.3.5), the additivity of Lelong numbers (proposition 12.1.4) shows that their sum has Lelong number $0$; hence $\nu (T_{\varepsilon /2}+T'_{\varepsilon /2}+\varepsilon \omega ,D)=0$.

The cone $\mathcal{MN}$ contains $\mathcal N$, since smooth forms have zero Lelong numbers, and it is contained in $\mathcal E$: if $\alpha $ is modified nef, then $\alpha +\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ contains the positive current $T_\varepsilon +\varepsilon \omega $, and $\mathcal E$ is closed. It is also closed. Indeed, let $\alpha _ j\to \alpha $ with $\alpha _ j\in \mathcal{MN}$, and let $\varepsilon >0$. By (14.16) we may choose $j$ such that $\alpha -\alpha _ j$ has a smooth representative $\eta $ with $-\varepsilon \omega /2\leq \eta \leq \varepsilon \omega /2$. If $T'\in \alpha _ j$ satisfies $T'\geq -\varepsilon \omega /2$ and $\nu (T'+\varepsilon \omega /2,D)=0$ for all $D$, then $T'+\eta \in \alpha $ satisfies $T'+\eta \geq -\varepsilon \omega $, and $\nu (T'+\eta +\varepsilon \omega ,D)=0$ for all $D$, since adding smooth forms does not change Lelong numbers.

As for the classes of definition 14.3.1, the definition of modified nef classes does not depend on $\omega $. Indeed, let $\omega '$ be another Kähler form and $C\geq 1$ with $\omega \leq C\omega '$ (lemma 13.1.1). For every $\varepsilon >0$ the current $T=T_{\varepsilon /C}$ satisfies $T\geq -(\varepsilon /C)\omega \geq -\varepsilon \omega '$, and the positive currents $T+\varepsilon \omega '$ and $T+(\varepsilon /C)\omega $ differ by a smooth form, so that $\nu (T+\varepsilon \omega ',D)=\nu (T+(\varepsilon /C)\omega ,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$.

In the definition of modified nef classes we may take $T_\varepsilon $ with gentle analytic singularities whose pole set has codimension at least two. Indeed, write $T_{\varepsilon /2}=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ with $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\varepsilon \omega /2)$ and apply lemma 13.4.1 to $\varphi $ with the background $\theta +\varepsilon \omega /2$, choosing $m$ with $c/m<\varepsilon /2$. Then $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq -\varepsilon \omega $. Since $\nu (\varphi ,D)=\nu (T_{\varepsilon /2}+\varepsilon \omega /2,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$, at a point $x$ of $D$ where $\nu (\varphi ,x)=\nu (\varphi ,D)$ the ideal $\mathcal I(m\varphi )_ x$ is the unit ideal by corollary 12.3.2. Hence the pole set of $G_ m$, which is the zero set of $\mathcal I(m\varphi )$, contains no prime divisor. It is not all of $X$, since $\mathcal I(m\varphi )_ x$ is the unit ideal at every point $x$ with $\varphi (x)>-\infty $, where $\nu (\varphi ,x)=0$ (proposition 12.1.1); so its irreducible components have codimension at least one, those of codimension one would be prime divisors, and it has codimension at least two. Moreover $G_ m$ is smooth near such a point $x$, so the Lelong numbers of $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m+\varepsilon \omega $ vanish there, and $\nu (\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m+\varepsilon \omega ,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$. Thus $T_\varepsilon =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m$ has the required properties.

Corollary 14.3.9. Let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, let $D_1,\ldots ,D_ N$ be distinct prime divisors and $a_1,\ldots ,a_ N>0$ real numbers, and put $D=\sum _ ia_ iD_ i$. Suppose that $\varphi $ has logarithmic singularities along $D$ with bounded remainder: near each point, $\varphi =\sum _ ia_ i\log |s_ i|^2+v$ with $v$ bounded, where $s_ i$ is a local equation of $D_ i$ (lemma A.2.16), and $s_ i=1$ if the point does not lie on $D_ i$. Then $\{ \theta \} -\{ D\} $ is nef, where $\{ D\} =\sum _ ia_ i\{ [D_ i]\} $. If $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a Kähler current, then $\{ \theta \} -\{ D\} $ is Kähler.

Proof. Near a point, let $h$ be smooth with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $. Off the support of $D$ the function $v+h=(\varphi +h)-\sum _ ia_ i\log |s_ i|^2$ is plurisubharmonic, since $\log |s_ i|^2$ is pluriharmonic there, and it is bounded above. On a connected coordinate neighborhood of the point, the support of $D$ lies in the zero set of the holomorphic function $\prod _ is_ i\not\equiv 0$, so theorem 8.4.1 extends $v+h$ to a plurisubharmonic function $\widetilde v$. The values of $\widetilde v$ on the support of $D$ are upper limits of values of $v+h$, so $\widetilde v$ is bounded, and $\widetilde v=v+h$ outside the null set $\operatorname{Supp}D$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |s_ i|^2=[D_ i]$ (lemma A.2.16), the current $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ satisfies $T-[D]=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde v\geq 0$ near the point, where $[D]=\sum _ ia_ i[D_ i]$. Thus $T-[D]$ is a positive current in $\{ \theta \} -\{ D\} $ with locally bounded potentials. If $T\geq \delta \omega $, apply the same argument to $T-\delta \omega $, whose potential has the same logarithmic singularities; then $T-[D]\geq \delta \omega $. In both cases proposition 14.3.5 gives the assertion. $\square$

14.4 Minimal multiplicities and positivity loci

Let $\alpha $ be a class in the pseudo-effective cone $\mathcal E$ and $\theta \in \alpha $ a smooth representative. For every $\varepsilon >0$ the class $\alpha +\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ is big, since it contains $T+\varepsilon \omega $ for every positive current $T\in \alpha $, and its potential $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }$ with minimal singularities measures the positivity of $\alpha $ that remains after the curvature is allowed to drop by $\varepsilon \omega $. The loci where positivity fails are described in theorem 14.4.4, and at the end of the section we compare positivity on $X$ and on a modification of $X$.

Definition 14.4.1. For $\alpha \in \mathcal E$, $x\in X$ and a prime divisor $D\subset X$ put

\[ \nu (\alpha ,x)=\sup _{\varepsilon >0}\nu (V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega },x), \qquad \nu (\alpha ,D)=\sup _{\varepsilon >0}\nu (V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega },D). \]

These are the minimal multiplicities of $\alpha $. The non-nef locus of $\alpha $ is

\[ E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )=\{ x\in X:\nu (\alpha ,x)>0\} . \]

If $\alpha $ is big, its non-Kähler locus is

\[ E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=\bigcap _ T\{ \varphi _ T=-\infty \} , \]

where $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ T$ ranges over the Kähler currents in $\alpha $ for which $\varphi _ T$ has analytic singularities. For a class $\alpha \in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ that is not big we put $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=X$, and for a class that is not pseudo-effective we put $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )=X$.

If $0<\varepsilon <\varepsilon '$, then $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\varepsilon \omega )\subset \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\varepsilon '\omega )$, so $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }\leq V_{\theta +\varepsilon '\omega }$. Hence the minimal potentials decrease as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, their Lelong numbers increase, and the suprema in the definition may be taken along $\varepsilon =1/j$. Replacing $\theta $ by another smooth representative $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$ changes $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }$ only by a bounded function, because $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }-g$ and $V_{\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g+\varepsilon \omega }$ both have minimal singularities in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g+\varepsilon \omega )$; and replacing $\omega $ by another Kähler form $\omega '$ with $C^{-1}\omega \leq \omega '\leq C\omega $ places $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega '}$ between $V_{\theta +C^{-1}\varepsilon \omega }$ and $V_{\theta +C\varepsilon \omega }$. Thus the minimal multiplicities and the two loci depend only on $\alpha $.

If $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a positive current in $\alpha $, then $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\varepsilon \omega )$ for every $\varepsilon >0$, so $\varphi \leq V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }+\sup _ X\varphi $; hence

\[ \nu (\alpha ,x)\leq \nu (T,x),\qquad \nu (\alpha ,D)\leq \nu (T,D), \]

and in particular both multiplicities are finite. For each $j$, the Lelong number $\nu (V_{\theta +\omega /j},x)$ equals its generic value $\nu (V_{\theta +\omega /j},D)$ at every point $x\in D$ outside a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $D$ (proposition 12.3.5 and the remarks before proposition 14.2.8). At points of $D$ outside the union of these countably many exceptional sets, which form a dense subset of $D$ by proposition 12.3.5, we therefore have $\nu (\alpha ,x)=\nu (\alpha ,D)$, while $\nu (\alpha ,x)\geq \nu (\alpha ,D)$ at every point of $D$. Consequently

\[ \nu (\alpha ,D)=\inf _{x\in D}\nu (\alpha ,x), \]

and the infimum is attained at every point of $D$ outside a countable union of proper analytic subsets.

Proposition 14.4.2. Let $\alpha \in \mathcal E$. Then $\nu (\alpha ,x)\leq \nu (V_\theta ,x)$ and $\nu (\alpha ,D)\leq \nu (V_\theta ,D)$ for every $x\in X$ and every prime divisor $D$, with equality if $\alpha $ is big. Moreover, $\alpha $ is modified nef if and only if $\nu (\alpha ,D)=0$ for every prime divisor $D$.

Proof. The positive current $T_0=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_\theta \in \alpha $ gives the inequalities, by the bound preceding the proposition. Suppose that $\alpha $ is big, and choose $S\in \alpha $ with $S\geq \delta \omega $, $\delta >0$. For $\varepsilon >0$ put $T_\varepsilon =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }\geq -\varepsilon \omega $ and $t=\varepsilon /(\delta +\varepsilon )$. Then $(1-t)T_\varepsilon +tS\geq \bigl (-(1-t)\varepsilon +t\delta \bigr )\omega =0$ is a positive current in $\alpha $, whose potential $(1-t)V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }+t\varphi _ S$, with $S=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ S$, is more singular than $V_\theta $. Since Lelong numbers are additive and positively homogeneous (proposition 12.1.4) and do not decrease when a function is replaced by a more singular one, we obtain

\[ \nu (V_\theta ,x)\leq (1-t)\nu (V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega },x)+t\nu (S,x)\leq (1-t)\nu (\alpha ,x)+t\nu (S,x). \]

As $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ we have $t\to 0$, and since $\nu (S,x)$ is finite we obtain $\nu (V_\theta ,x)\leq \nu (\alpha ,x)$. For a prime divisor $D$, evaluate the same inequality at a point $x\in D$ at which the Lelong numbers of $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }$ and of $S$ attain their generic values along $D$ (proposition 12.3.5); it gives $\nu (V_\theta ,D)\leq \nu (V_\theta ,x)\leq (1-t)\nu (\alpha ,D)+t\nu (S,D)$, and letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ proves the equality for $D$.

If $\alpha $ is modified nef and $T_\varepsilon $ is as in definition 14.3.8, then $T_\varepsilon =\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _\varepsilon $ with $\varphi _\varepsilon \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +\varepsilon \omega )$, so $\nu (V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega },D)\leq \nu (\varphi _\varepsilon ,D)=0$ for every $\varepsilon >0$; hence $\nu (\alpha ,D)=0$. Conversely, if $\nu (\alpha ,D)=0$ for every $D$, then the currents $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }$ satisfy the conditions of definition 14.3.8. $\square$

Proposition 14.4.3. For a fixed point $x\in X$ or a fixed prime divisor $D$, the minimal multiplicity $\alpha \mapsto \nu (\alpha ,x)$, respectively $\alpha \mapsto \nu (\alpha ,D)$, is positively homogeneous, convex and lower semicontinuous on the pseudo-effective cone $\mathcal E$, and continuous on the big cone $\mathcal B$.

Proof. We treat $\nu (\cdot ,x)$. The proof for $D$ is the same, except that the additivity of Lelong numbers along $D$ is applied at a point of $D$ at which all the functions involved attain their generic Lelong numbers along $D$ (proposition 12.3.5). For $t>0$ we have $\mathrm{PSH}(X,t\theta +\varepsilon \omega )=t\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta +(\varepsilon /t)\omega )$, hence $V_{t\theta +\varepsilon \omega }=tV_{\theta +(\varepsilon /t)\omega }$, and taking suprema over $\varepsilon $ gives $\nu (t\alpha ,x)=t\nu (\alpha ,x)$. If $\alpha _1,\alpha _2\in \mathcal E$ have representatives $\theta _1,\theta _2$, the sum of the minimal currents with allowance $\varepsilon $ in the two classes is a current $\theta _1+\theta _2+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(V_{\theta _1+\varepsilon \omega }+V_{\theta _2+\varepsilon \omega })\geq -2\varepsilon \omega $ in $\alpha _1+\alpha _2$. By minimality and additivity of Lelong numbers,

\[ \nu (V_{\theta _1+\theta _2+2\varepsilon \omega },x)\leq \nu (V_{\theta _1+\varepsilon \omega },x)+\nu (V_{\theta _2+\varepsilon \omega },x)\leq \nu (\alpha _1,x)+\nu (\alpha _2,x), \]

and taking the supremum over $\varepsilon $ gives subadditivity. Together with homogeneity this proves convexity.

Let $\alpha _ j\to \alpha $ in $\mathcal E$, with representatives $\theta _ j$, and fix $\varepsilon >0$. For large $j$, by (14.16), the class $\alpha -\alpha _ j$ has a smooth representative $\eta _ j$ with $-\varepsilon \omega \leq \eta _ j\leq \varepsilon \omega $. The current $\theta _ j+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}V_{\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega }+\eta _ j\in \alpha $ is at least $-2\varepsilon \omega $, and its potential relative to $\theta $ differs from $V_{\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega }$ by a smooth function. By minimality,

\[ \nu (V_{\theta +2\varepsilon \omega },x)\leq \nu (V_{\theta _ j+\varepsilon \omega },x)\leq \nu (\alpha _ j,x) \]

for large $j$, so $\nu (V_{\theta +2\varepsilon \omega },x)\leq \varliminf _ j\nu (\alpha _ j,x)$. Taking the supremum over $\varepsilon $ proves lower semicontinuity.

Finally, a convex function $f$ with finite values on an open convex subset $U$ of a finite-dimensional real vector space is continuous. Indeed, let $Q\subset U$ be a small closed cube; by convexity the function is bounded above on $Q$ by the maximum $M$ of its values at the vertices. If $c$ is the center of $Q$ and $c\pm v\in Q$, then $f(c+v)\geq 2f(c)-f(c-v)\geq 2f(c)-M$, so $f$ is also bounded below on $Q$. If $y,z$ lie in the concentric cube of half the size, extend the segment from $z$ to $y$ beyond $y$ to the point $w$ at distance $s$ from $y$, where $s$ is half the side length of the smaller cube, so that $w\in Q$. The slope inequality for convex functions gives $f(y)-f(z)\leq |y-z|(f(w)-f(y))/|w-y|\leq |y-z|(M-\inf _ Qf)/s$, and by symmetry $f$ is Lipschitz on the smaller cube. Applied to the open convex cone $\mathcal B$, this proves continuity there. $\square$

Theorem 14.4.4. Let $\alpha \in \mathcal E$. Then the non-nef locus $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )$ is a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $X$, and it is empty if and only if $\alpha $ is nef. If $\alpha $ is big, then $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$ is the pole set of the potential of a single Kähler current in $\alpha $ with analytic singularities; it is a proper analytic subset of $X$, it contains $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )$, and it is empty if and only if $\alpha $ is Kähler. Finally, for every class $\alpha \in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$,

14.21
\begin{equation} E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )= \bigcap _{\varepsilon >0}E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ). \end{equation}

Proof. The non-nef locus. By the monotonicity in $\varepsilon $, $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )$ is the union over positive integers $j,k$ of the sets $\{ x:\nu (V_{\theta +\omega /j},x)\geq 1/k\} $. Each of them is analytic by theorem 12.3.4, and proper, because it is contained in the pole set of $V_{\theta +\omega /j}$. If $\alpha $ is nef, then $\alpha +\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ is Kähler for every $\varepsilon >0$, so $V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega }$ is bounded by corollary 14.3.6 and all these Lelong numbers vanish. Conversely, suppose that $\nu (V_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega },x)=0$ for all $x\in X$ and $\varepsilon >0$. By corollary 12.3.2, $\mathcal I(mV_{\theta +\varepsilon \omega })$ is the unit ideal at every point, so the approximations $G_ m$ of lemma 13.4.1 for the background $\theta +\varepsilon \omega $ are smooth and satisfy $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq -(\varepsilon +c/m)\omega $. Hence $\alpha $ is nef.

Realization of the non-Kähler locus. Let $\alpha $ be big. Kähler currents with analytic singularities exist in $\alpha $ by proposition 14.3.5, and their pole sets are closed analytic subsets of $X$. Since $X$ has a countable basis, countably many of the open complements of these pole sets already have the same union as all of them: for every element of a countable basis that is contained in one of these complements, choose one such complement. The intersection of the corresponding countably many pole sets $Z_1,Z_2,\ldots $ is therefore $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$. Let $\mathcal I_{Z_ k}$ be the ideal sheaf of $Z_ k$, which is coherent by theorem A.2.8. The increasing sequence of coherent ideals $\mathcal I_{Z_1}+\cdots +\mathcal I_{Z_ k}$, whose zero sets are $Z_1\cap \cdots \cap Z_ k$, is stationary on the compact manifold $X$ by lemma 12.2.4. Hence $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=Z_1\cap \cdots \cap Z_ N$ for some $N$.

Let $T_ k=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ k$, $k\leq N$, be the corresponding Kähler currents, and choose $\delta >0$ with $T_ k\geq \delta \omega $ for all $k\leq N$. Then $\varphi =\max _ k\varphi _ k$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta -\delta \omega )$, it has analytic singularities by proposition 13.1.7, and its pole set is exactly $Z_1\cap \cdots \cap Z_ N=E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$. Thus $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a Kähler current realizing $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$, which is a proper analytic subset because $\varphi $ is finite almost everywhere. If $\nu (\alpha ,x)>0$, then $\nu (\varphi ,x)\geq \nu (V_\theta ,x)=\nu (\alpha ,x)>0$ by minimality of $V_\theta $ and proposition 14.4.2, so $\varphi (x)=-\infty $; hence $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )\subset E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$.

If $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=\varnothing $, the potential $\varphi $ just constructed has analytic singularities and no poles, so it is bounded, and proposition 14.3.5 shows that $\alpha $ is Kähler. Conversely, if $\alpha $ contains a Kähler form $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$, then $u$ is smooth and has analytic singularities without poles, so $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=\varnothing $.

The identity (14.21). Suppose first that $x\notin E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$. Then $\alpha $ is big, and there is a Kähler current $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \omega $ with analytic singularities and $\varphi (x)>-\infty $; in particular $\varphi $ is bounded near $x$ and $\nu (\varphi ,x)=0$. For $0<\varepsilon <\delta $, the current $\theta -\varepsilon \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is positive and lies in $\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $, which is therefore pseudo-effective, and the bound $\nu (\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ,x)\leq \nu (\varphi ,x)=0$ shows that $x\notin E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} )$.

Conversely, suppose that $x\notin E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} )$ for some $\varepsilon >0$. By the convention in definition 14.4.1, the class $\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} $ is pseudo-effective. For $0<\eta <\varepsilon /2$ put $u=V_{\theta -(\varepsilon -\eta )\omega }$; then $\nu (u,x)\leq \nu (\alpha -\varepsilon \{ \omega \} ,x)=0$. By corollary 12.3.2, $\mathcal I(mu)$ is the unit ideal near $x$ for every $m$, and the approximation $G_ m$ of lemma 13.4.1 for $u$ and the background $\theta -(\varepsilon -\eta )\omega $ is smooth near $x$ and satisfies

\[ \theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}G_ m\geq (\varepsilon -\eta -c/m)\omega . \]

For large $m$ this is a Kähler current in $\alpha $ with analytic singularities whose potential is finite at $x$. Hence $\alpha $ is big and $x\notin E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )$. This proves (14.21); the argument also shows that $\alpha $ is big as soon as the right-hand side of (14.21) is not all of $X$, so the formula is consistent with the convention $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=X$ for classes that are not big. $\square$

We now turn to modifications. Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be a proper bimeromorphic holomorphic map from a Kähler manifold $Y$, with Kähler form $\omega _ Y$. By proposition 14.2.8, $Y$ is compact and connected, so the results above apply on $Y$. Pullback of smooth forms induces a linear map $\pi ^*\colon H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)\to H^{1,1}(Y,\mathbb R)$. Pushforward of currents, $(\pi _*S)(\chi )=S(\pi ^*\chi )$, maps closed real $(1,1)$-currents on $Y$ to closed real $(1,1)$-currents on $X$, preserves positivity, and satisfies $\pi _*\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\pi _*f$ for distributions $f$ (proposition 7.5.1); hence it induces a linear map $\pi _*\colon H^{1,1}(Y,\mathbb R)\to H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ with $\pi _*\{ S\} =\{ \pi _*S\} $. If $T=\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a closed positive current on $X$ with $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\theta )$, the pullbacks $(\varphi +h)\circ \pi $ of its local potentials, where $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=\theta $, are not identically $-\infty $, because $\varphi \circ \pi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\pi ^*\theta )$ by proposition 14.2.8. Hence the pullback $\pi ^*T$ of proposition 7.5.4 is defined, and

\[ \pi ^*T=\pi ^*\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi \circ \pi ),\qquad \{ \pi ^*T\} =\pi ^*\{ T\} . \]

For a prime divisor $E\subset Y$, the image $\pi (E)$ is an analytic subset of $X$ by Remmert's theorem (theorem A.6.2), and it is irreducible: if $\pi (E)=A_1\cup A_2$ with analytic subsets $A_1,A_2$, then $E=(E\cap \pi ^{-1}(A_1))\cup (E\cap \pi ^{-1}(A_2))$, so $E\subset \pi ^{-1}(A_ i)$ for some $i$ and $\pi (E)=A_ i$. The divisor $E$ is exceptional for $\pi $ if $\pi (E)$ has codimension at least two in $X$.

Fix an analytic subset $A\subset X$ of codimension at least two outside which $\pi $ is an isomorphism (proposition A.7.3). We show that a prime divisor $E$ of $Y$ is exceptional if and only if $E\subset \pi ^{-1}(A)$. If $E\subset \pi ^{-1}(A)$, then $\pi (E)\subset A$ and $E$ is exceptional. Otherwise $E\cap \pi ^{-1}(A)$ is an analytic subset of $E$ different from $E$; it contains no nonempty open subset of the connected manifold $E_{\mathrm{reg}}$, by the identity theorem, so it is nowhere dense in $E$. Then $\pi $ maps the dense open subset $E\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$ of $E$, a complex hypersurface of $Y\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$, isomorphically onto $\pi (E)\setminus A$, which is therefore a complex hypersurface of $X\setminus A$ and is dense in $\pi (E)$, because $\pi (E)$ is the image of the closure of $E\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$ under the closed map $\pi $. Hence $\pi (E)$ is an irreducible analytic subset of codimension one, that is, a prime divisor $D$, and $E$ is not exceptional; this proves the claim. Moreover, $E$ is the strict transform of $D$, since the closure of $\pi ^{-1}(D\setminus A)=E\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$ is $E$. By the claim and (14.20), applied on $Y$, every exceptional prime divisor is an irreducible component of $\pi ^{-1}(A)$, while every other prime divisor $E$ of $Y$ is the strict transform of the prime divisor $\pi (E)$ of $X$, and $\pi $ is an isomorphism near the points of $E$ outside $\pi ^{-1}(A)$.

Proposition 14.4.5. Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be as above. Then $\pi ^*$ maps nef classes to nef classes, and $\pi _*$ maps modified nef classes to modified nef classes. The pushforward of a non-divisorial closed positive current on $Y$ is non-divisorial.

Proof. By lemma 13.1.1 on the compact manifold $Y$, $\pi ^*\omega \leq C\omega _ Y$ for a constant $C$. If $\theta _\varepsilon \geq -\varepsilon \omega $ are nef representatives of a class on $X$, then $\pi ^*\theta _\varepsilon \geq -C\varepsilon \omega _ Y$, so the pullback class is nef. Let $S$ be a non-divisorial closed positive current on $Y$ and $D\subset X$ a prime divisor, with strict transform $D'$. Over $X\setminus A$ the map $\pi $ is a biholomorphism, which identifies $\pi _*S$ with $S$ and $D\setminus A$ with $D'\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$, and Lelong numbers are invariant under biholomorphisms (proposition 12.1.1). The generic value of the Lelong numbers of $S$ along $D'$ is attained on a dense subset of $D'$ (proposition 12.3.5), hence at some point $y$ of the nonempty open subset $D'\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$. Therefore $\nu (\pi _*S,D)\leq \nu (\pi _*S,\pi (y))=\nu (S,y)=\nu (S,D')=0$. Hence $\pi _*S$ is non-divisorial.

Let $\beta $ be a modified nef class on $Y$ and $t>0$. The class $\beta +t\{ \omega _ Y\} $ is big, and it is modified nef because $\mathcal{MN}$ is a convex cone containing the Kähler classes. By proposition 14.4.2, a positive current with minimal singularities in this class is non-divisorial. Its pushforward is a non-divisorial positive current in $\pi _*\beta +t\pi _*\{ \omega _ Y\} $, so this class is modified nef: the definition is satisfied with $T_\varepsilon $ equal to this current for every $\varepsilon $. These classes tend to $\pi _*\beta $ as $t\downarrow 0$, and $\mathcal{MN}$ is closed, so $\pi _*\beta $ is modified nef. $\square$

Lemma 14.4.6. Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be as above, and let $S$ be a non-divisorial closed positive $(1,1)$-current on $Y$. Then

\[ \pi ^*\pi _*S=S+\sum _ E c_ E[E],\qquad c_ E\geq 0, \]

where $E$ ranges over the finitely many exceptional prime divisors of $\pi $. For every closed positive $(1,1)$-current $T$ on $X$, $\pi _*\pi ^*T=T$. For a prime divisor $E$ on $Y$, $\pi _*[E]=0$ if $E$ is exceptional, and $\pi _*[E]=[\pi (E)]$ otherwise.

Proof. Let $A$ be as above. The positive current $\pi ^*\pi _*S$, defined through local potentials as above, agrees with $S$ on $Y\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$. Their difference is a closed real current with measure coefficients supported on the proper analytic subset $\pi ^{-1}(A)$ of $Y$. By theorem A.2.17, it is a finite real linear combination of the currents of integration along the irreducible components of codimension one of the compact analytic set $\pi ^{-1}(A)$, which are exactly the exceptional prime divisors, as shown above; there are finitely many of them by corollary A.2.13.

It remains to show that the coefficients $c_ E$ are nonnegative. Near a regular point of an exceptional prime divisor $E$ that lies on no other component, the difference is $c_ E[E]=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(c_ E\log |s_ E|^2)$, where $s_ E$ is a local equation of $E$ (lemma A.2.16), so local potentials $u'$ of $\pi ^*\pi _*S$ and $u$ of $S$ satisfy $u'=u+c_ E\log |s_ E|^2+p$ with $p$ pluriharmonic. The additivity of Lelong numbers (proposition 12.1.4), applied to this identity if $c_ E\geq 0$ and to $u=u'+|c_ E|\log |s_ E|^2-p$ otherwise, together with $\nu (\log |s_ E|^2,y)=1$, gives $\nu (u',y)=\nu (u,y)+c_ E$ at such points $y$. At points that are moreover generic for both currents, which exist because the points of $E$ excluded by these conditions form a countable union of proper analytic subsets of $E$ (proposition 12.3.5), this reads $\nu (\pi ^*\pi _*S,E)=\nu (S,E)+c_ E$; hence $c_ E=\nu (\pi ^*\pi _*S,E)\geq 0$, since $S$ is non-divisorial.

Since $\pi $ is a biholomorphism over $X\setminus A$, the positive current $\pi _*\pi ^*T$ agrees with $T$ on $X\setminus A$. Hence $\pi _*\pi ^*T-T$ is a closed real current with measure coefficients (theorem 7.2.5) supported on $A$, which has codimension at least two, so it vanishes by theorem A.2.17. For a prime divisor $E$ on $Y$, the current $\pi _*[E]$ is positive, closed and supported on the analytic set $\pi (E)$. If $E$ is exceptional, $\pi (E)$ has codimension at least two and $\pi _*[E]=0$ by the same theorem. Otherwise $\pi $ maps $E\setminus \pi ^{-1}(A)$ isomorphically onto $\pi (E)\setminus A$, so $\pi _*[E]=[\pi (E)]$ on $X\setminus A$, and the closed current $\pi _*[E]-[\pi (E)]$ of order zero is supported on $\pi (E)\cap A$, of codimension at least two in $X$; it vanishes by the same theorem. $\square$

Proposition 14.4.7. Let $\pi \colon Y\to X$ be as above. Then $\pi ^*$ and $\pi _*$ map big classes to big classes.

Proof. Choose $C$ with $\pi ^*\omega \leq C\omega _ Y$. Since $\pi _*$ preserves positivity, lemma 14.4.6 gives $\pi _*\omega _ Y\geq C^{-1}\pi _*\pi ^*\omega =C^{-1}\omega $. Hence a current $S\geq \delta \omega _ Y$ on $Y$ has $\pi _*S\geq \delta C^{-1}\omega $, which proves the assertion for $\pi _*$.

Let $\alpha $ be big on $X$ and $T\in \alpha $ with $T\geq \delta \omega $. Choose a smooth closed representative $\eta $ of the class $\{ \pi _*\omega _ Y\} $, a constant $B>0$ with $\eta \leq B\omega $ (lemma 13.1.1), and $t>0$ with $tB\leq \delta $, so that $T-t\eta \geq 0$. The positive current $T-t\eta +t\pi _*\omega _ Y$ lies in $\alpha $. By lemma 14.4.6, applied to the smooth, hence non-divisorial, current $\omega _ Y$, its pullback is

\[ \pi ^*(T-t\eta )+t\omega _ Y+t\sum _ E c_ E[E]\geq t\omega _ Y. \]

This is a Kähler current in $\pi ^*\alpha $, so $\pi ^*\alpha $ is big. $\square$

Example 14.4.8. The zero class $\alpha =0$ is nef, so $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(0)=\varnothing $ by theorem 14.4.4. It is not big: a Kähler current $T\geq \delta \omega $ would have $\int _ XT\wedge \omega ^{n-1}\geq \delta \int _ X\omega ^ n>0$, whereas this integral vanishes for every closed current in the zero class. Hence $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(0)=X$. More generally, $E_{\mathrm{nK}}(\alpha )=X$ for every class that is not big, while $E_{\mathrm{nn}}(\alpha )=\varnothing $ for every nef class; on the boundary of the pseudo-effective cone the two loci therefore detect different failures of positivity. This is also why the minimal multiplicities are defined with the allowances $\varepsilon \omega $ rather than through $V_\theta $: for a class that is not big, proposition 14.4.2 gives only the inequality $\nu (\alpha ,x)\leq \nu (V_\theta ,x)$, since the proof of equality uses a Kähler current in $\alpha $.

14.5 Curvature and metrics defined by sections

This section collects the notions for line bundles on $X$ used in the rest of the chapter, from the curvature class to the Fubini–Study metrics defined by holomorphic sections, and shows that spaces of global holomorphic sections are finite dimensional.

Let $L\to X$ be a holomorphic line bundle, given as in definition 13.1.8 by frames $e_ i$ over an open cover $(V_ i)$ of $X$, and let $h_0$ be a smooth quadratic Hermitian metric on $L$. Its curvature form, which we denote by $c_1(L,h_0)$, equals $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |e_ i|_{h_0}^2$ on $V_ i$. It also equals $-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |e|_{h_0}^2$ for every local holomorphic frame $e$ of $L$: on $V_ i$ we have $e=ge_ i$ with $g$ holomorphic and nowhere zero, so $\log |e|_{h_0}^2=\log |g|^2+\log |e_ i|_{h_0}^2$, and $\log |g|^2$ is pluriharmonic (section 7.3). Any other smooth metric is $h_1=h_0\mathrm e^{-f}$ with $f$ smooth and real, and then $c_1(L,h_1)=c_1(L,h_0)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f$ (lemma 13.1.9(3)). Hence the class $c_1(L)=\{ c_1(L,h_0)\} \in H^{1,1}(X,\mathbb R)$ does not depend on the metric. A holomorphic section of $L$ is given by holomorphic functions $f_ i$ on $V_ i$ with $f_ ie_ i=f_ je_ j$ on $V_ i\cap V_ j$.

If $L$ and $L'$ are given by frames $e_ i$ and $e'_ i$ over a common cover, with transition functions $g_{ij}$ and $g'_{ij}$, their tensor product $L\otimes L'$ is given by the frames $e_ i\otimes e'_ i$ with the transition functions $g_{ij}g'_{ij}$, and the dual $L^{-1}$ of $L$ by the dual frames $e_ i^*$ with the transition functions $g_{ij}^{-1}$. Metrics on $L$ and $L'$ induce metrics with $|e_ i\otimes e'_ i|^2=|e_ i|^2|e'_ i|^2$ and $|e_ i^*|^2=|e_ i|^{-2}$. The curvature of a tensor product of metrics is the sum of the curvatures, and the curvature of a pullback metric is the pullback of the curvature, because the logarithms of the squared norms of local frames add, respectively compose.

Line bundles are trivial over coordinate balls; we use this to work in a single frame on balls of a fixed size.

Lemma 14.5.1. Let $L$ be a holomorphic line bundle on a complex manifold $M$, and let $B$ be a coordinate ball whose closure is contained in the domain of its chart. Then $L$ has a holomorphic frame over $B$, that is, a nowhere vanishing holomorphic section of $L$ on $B$.

Proof. We make the metric flat on $B$ and obtain the frame by parallel transport along the segments issuing from the center of $B$. Choose a smooth metric $h_0$ on $L$ (lemma 13.1.9(1)). By lemma 13.1.2 there is a smooth real function $p$ on $B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p=c_1(L,h_0)$, and the metric $h_1=h_0\mathrm e^{p}$ on $L|_ B$ has curvature $c_1(L,h_0)-\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}p=0$, by lemma 13.1.9(3). We call a holomorphic frame of $h_1$-length one over an open subset of $B$ a unit frame. Unit frames exist near every point of $B$. Indeed, every point of $B$ has a ball neighborhood $V\subset B$ over which $L$ has a holomorphic frame $e$, and $\log |e|_{h_1}^2$ is smooth and pluriharmonic, since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log |e|_{h_1}^2=-c_1(L,h_1)=0$, so by lemma 13.1.2 it equals $\operatorname{Re}F$ for a holomorphic function $F$ on $V$, and $\mathrm e^{-F/2}e$ is a unit frame. Two unit frames on a connected open set differ by a holomorphic factor $g$ with $|g|=1$; then $\bar g=1/g$ is both holomorphic and antiholomorphic, so $\mathrm d\bar g=0$ and $g$ is constant.

Let $c$ be the center of $B$ and fix a unit vector $e_ c\in L_ c$. For $z\in B$, a flat lift along the segment from $c$ to $z$ is a continuous map $t\mapsto \varepsilon (t)\in L_{c+t(z-c)}$, $t\in [0,1]$, with $\varepsilon (0)=e_ c$, which near every $t$ is a constant multiple of a unit frame. There is at most one flat lift: if $\varepsilon '$ is another one, then $\varepsilon '=g\varepsilon $ for a continuous function $g$ on $[0,1]$ which is locally constant, by the preceding paragraph, and equals $1$ at $t=0$, so $g=1$.

It remains to show that flat lifts exist and that their values at $t=1$ depend holomorphically on $z$. Let $z_0\in B$, and choose $\delta _0>0$ with $\overline{B(z_0,\delta _0)}\subset B$. The set $\Gamma _0=\{ c+t(z-c):t\in [0,1],\ |z-z_0|\leq \delta _0\} $ is a compact subset of the convex set $B$. Cover it by finitely many balls $V_ i\subset B$ carrying unit frames $e_ i$, let $\lambda >0$ be a Lebesgue number of this cover, choose $\delta \leq \delta _0$ with $2\delta <\lambda /2$, and choose $0=t_0<t_1<\cdots <t_ N=1$ with $(t_ k-t_{k-1})(|z_0-c|+\delta _0)<\lambda /2$. For $W=B(z_0,\delta )$, each set $\Gamma _ k=\{ c+t(z-c):t\in [t_{k-1},t_ k],\ z\in W\} $ has diameter less than $\lambda $, because $|(c+t(z-c))-(c+t'(z'-c))|\leq |t-t'|\, |z-c|+|z-z'|$; so $\Gamma _ k$ lies in some $V_{i_ k}$. The convex set $V_{i_ k}\cap V_{i_{k+1}}$ is connected and contains $c+t_ k(z_0-c)$, so $e_{i_ k}=\gamma _ ke_{i_{k+1}}$ there for a constant $\gamma _ k$. Let $a$ be the number with $e_ c=a\, e_{i_1}(c)$. For $z\in W$, the map equal to $a\gamma _1\cdots \gamma _{k-1}e_{i_ k}(c+t(z-c))$ for $t\in [t_{k-1},t_ k]$ is well defined at the points $t_ k$ and is a flat lift along the segment from $c$ to $z$. Hence, on $W$, the value $E(z)$ of the unique flat lift at $t=1$ is $a\gamma _1\cdots \gamma _{N-1}e_{i_ N}(z)$, a constant multiple of the unit frame $e_{i_ N}$. Since $z_0$ was arbitrary, $E$ is a nowhere vanishing holomorphic section of $L$ on $B$. $\square$

Families of holomorphic sections that are bounded in $L^2$ are relatively compact for uniform convergence.

Lemma 14.5.2.

  1. Let $U\subset \mathbb C^ n$ be open, $n\geq 1$, and let $(f_ j)$ be a sequence of holomorphic functions on $U$ with $\sup _ j\int _ U|f_ j|^2\, \mathrm dV<\infty $. Then a subsequence converges locally uniformly on $U$ to a holomorphic function.

  2. Let $E$ be a holomorphic line bundle on $X$ with a smooth quadratic metric $h_ E$, and let $(s_ j)$ be a sequence of holomorphic sections of $E$ over $X$ with $\sup _ j\int _ X|s_ j|_{h_ E}^2\, \mathrm dV<\infty $. Then a subsequence converges uniformly on $X$ to a holomorphic section $s$ of $E$, that is, $\sup _ X|s_ j-s|_{h_ E}\to 0$ along the subsequence.

  3. For every holomorphic line bundle $E$ on $X$, the space $H^0(X,E)$ of holomorphic sections of $E$ over $X$ is finite dimensional.

Assertions (2) and (3) hold more generally on every compact complex manifold $X'$ of positive dimension in place of $X$, with $\mathrm dV$ in (2) replaced by any smooth positive volume form on $X'$.

Proof. (1) Let $M$ be the supremum in the statement. If $\overline{B(z,\delta )}\subset U$, then $|f_ j(z)|^2\leq |B(z,\delta )|^{-1}M$ by (12.9). Hence the $f_ j$ are uniformly bounded on every compact subset of $U$: if $K\subset U$ is compact and $\delta >0$ is smaller than the distance from $K$ to $\mathbb C^ n\setminus U$ (any $\delta >0$ if $U=\mathbb C^ n$), then $|f_ j|^2\leq |B(0,\delta )|^{-1}M$ on $K$.

We next show that the sequence is equicontinuous on compact sets. Let $K\subset U$ be compact, and choose $r>0$ such that the compact set $K'$ of points at distance at most $2\sqrt n\, r$ from $K$ lies in $U$; let $M'$ be a bound for all $|f_ j|$ on $K'$. For $z$ at distance at most $\sqrt n\, r$ from $K$, the closed polydisc of radius $r$ centered at $z$ lies in $K'$, and the coefficient bound in (7.1) gives $|\partial _{z_ l}f_ j(z)|\leq M'/r$ for $1\leq l\leq n$. Since $\mathrm df_ j=\sum _ l\partial _{z_ l}f_ j\, \mathrm dz_ l$, the $f_ j$ are Lipschitz with a common constant on every ball of radius $\sqrt n\, r$ centered at a point of $K$, so the sequence is equicontinuous on $K$. By proposition 1.6.4, applied on the compact sets $K_ m=\{ z\in U:|z|\leq m,\ \operatorname {dist}(z,\mathbb C^ n\setminus U)\geq 1/m\} $, which exhaust $U$, and a diagonal argument, a subsequence converges locally uniformly on $U$ to a continuous function $f$. Then $\bar\partial f=0$ in the sense of distributions, as a limit of $\bar\partial f_ j=0$, so $f$ agrees almost everywhere with a holomorphic function by lemma 11.3.5(1); both being continuous, $f$ is holomorphic.

(2) Choose finitely many coordinate balls $B_ k$, with closures in the domains of their charts, such that the concentric balls $B'_ k$ of half the radius cover $X$, and let $B''_ k$ be the concentric balls of three quarters of the radius. By lemma 14.5.1, $E$ has a holomorphic frame $e_ k$ over $B_ k$; write $s_ j=f_{j,k}e_ k$ on $B_ k$. On the compact set $\overline{B''_ k}$ the function $|e_ k|_{h_ E}^2$ is bounded below by a positive constant and $\mathrm dV$ is bounded below by a positive multiple of Lebesgue measure in the chart, so the integrals of $|f_{j,k}|^2$ over $B''_ k$ with respect to Lebesgue measure in the chart are bounded independently of $j$. Applying (1) successively for the finitely many $k$, we obtain a subsequence along which $f_{j,k}$ converges locally uniformly on $B''_ k$, hence uniformly on $\overline{B'_ k}$, to a holomorphic function $f_ k$, for every $k$. On $B''_ k\cap B''_ l$ the sections $f_ ke_ k$ and $f_ le_ l$ are both the pointwise limit of $s_ j$, so they define a holomorphic section $s$ of $E$ over $X$, and $\sup _{B'_ k}|s_ j-s|_{h_ E}\leq \sup _{\overline{B'_ k}}|e_ k|_{h_ E}\cdot \sup _{\overline{B'_ k}}|f_{j,k}-f_ k|\to 0$ for every $k$.

(3) Fix a smooth quadratic metric $h_ E$ on $E$ (lemma 13.1.9(1)). The integral $\| s\| ^2=\int _ X|s|_{h_ E}^2\, \mathrm dV$ defines an inner product on $H^0(X,E)$: if $\| s\| =0$, then the continuous function $|s|_{h_ E}$ vanishes almost everywhere, hence everywhere. If $H^0(X,E)$ were infinite dimensional, the Gram–Schmidt process would give an orthonormal sequence $(s_ j)$. By (2), a subsequence converges uniformly on $X$, hence in $L^2$, since $X$ has finite volume; but $\| s_ i-s_ j\| ^2=2$ for $i\neq j$. This contradiction proves (3).

The proofs of (2) and (3) use only that $X$ is a compact complex manifold of positive dimension and that $\mathrm dV$ is a smooth positive volume form; hence they also prove the last assertion of the lemma. $\square$

A line bundle is positive if it admits a smooth metric with positive definite curvature form. It is very ample if its full space of global holomorphic sections defines a holomorphic embedding into a projective space: this space is finite dimensional by lemma 14.5.2(3), and for a basis $s_0,\ldots ,s_ N$ we require that the sections have no common zero and that $x\mapsto [s_0(x):\cdots :s_ N(x)]$ is a holomorphic embedding of $X$ into $\mathbb P^ N$.
