For a prime divisor $D$ on the final model, put $b_ D=\operatorname {ord}_ D(E)$, the vanishing order along $D$ of the pullback of a local equation of $E$, and $A_ D=A_ Z(D)$. We call $A_ D-b_ D$ the margin of $D$.

Lemma 14.7.5. In this situation, $A_ D-b_ D=0$ if $D$ is the strict transform of $E$, and $A_ D-b_ D>0$ for every other prime divisor $D$ in the boundary.

Proof. We argue by induction on the number of blowups. On $Z$ itself, $A_ E=b_ E=1$, and every other prime divisor $D$ of $Z$ has $A_ D=1$ and $b_ D=0$. Strict transforms keep their log discrepancies and orders, hence their margins. Since every exceptional divisor lies in the boundary, a prime divisor of a model outside the boundary is the strict transform of a prime divisor of $Z$ other than $E$, so it has $A_ D=1$ and $b_ D=0$. Suppose that the statement holds on a model. Blowing up a center of codimension one is an isomorphism and creates no new divisor; it only adds the center to the boundary, and the center then has margin one if it was not a boundary component. It therefore suffices to show that the exceptional divisor $E_{\mathrm{new}}$ of the blowup of a center $C$ of codimension $c\geq 2$ has a positive margin.

Suppose that $C$ is contained in exactly $r$ boundary components, near its generic point. Since $C$ has normal crossings with the boundary, $r\leq c$. Near such a point choose coordinates $(z,w)$, $w=(w_1,\ldots ,w_ c)$, in which $C=\{ w=0\} $ and the boundary components through the point are coordinate hyperplanes; those containing $C$ are then hyperplanes $\{ w_ j=0\} $. By proposition A.5.3, in the coordinates $(z,t,v)$ of (A.8) we have $E_{\mathrm{new}}=\{ t=0\} $, and the Jacobian determinant of the blowup is $\pm t^{c-1}$, so it vanishes to order $c-1$ along $E_{\mathrm{new}}$. A local equation of a component $\{ w_ j=0\} $ containing $C$ is a unit times $w_ j$ (lemma 12.6.1(1)), and $w_ j$ pulls back to $t$ or to $tv_ j$, which vanishes to order one along $E_{\mathrm{new}}$, because $v_ j$ does not vanish identically on $\{ t=0\} $ (lemma 12.6.1(2),(4)). Thus the pullback of a local equation of each component containing $C$ vanishes to order one along $E_{\mathrm{new}}$. Hence $A_{\mathrm{new}}=c+\sum _{D\supset C}(A_ D-1)$ and $b_{\mathrm{new}}=\sum _{D\supset C}b_ D$, the sums running over the boundary components containing $C$ (other divisors through $C$ have $A_ D=1$ and $b_ D=0$ and contribute nothing). Therefore

\[ A_{\mathrm{new}}-b_{\mathrm{new}} =c-r+\sum _{D\supset C}(A_ D-b_ D). \]

All terms on the right are nonnegative by the induction hypothesis. If one of the components containing $C$ has a positive margin, so does $E_{\mathrm{new}}$. Otherwise only the strict transform of $E$ can contain $C$, so $r\leq 1$ and $c-r\geq 1$, and again the margin of $E_{\mathrm{new}}$ is positive. $\square$

Lemma 14.7.6. Let $L\to X$ be a holomorphic line bundle with a smooth quadratic metric $h_0$ and a plurisubharmonic metric $h=h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi }$. Let $Y$ be a nonempty connected closed complex submanifold of codimension $q\geq 1$ such that the restriction of $h$ to $Y$ is defined, and let $R_ Y$ be as in lemma 14.7.4, with $\max _ XR_ Y<\mathrm e^{-4}$. If $f$ is a holomorphic section of $L|_ Y$ with $\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y<\infty $, there are $\kappa >0$, finitely many open sets $U_ i\subset X$, holomorphic sections $F_ i$ of $L$ over $U_ i$ with $F_ i=f$ on $Y\cap U_ i$, and smooth functions $\eta _ i$ with compact support in $U_ i$ and $\sum _ i\eta _ i=1$ on a neighborhood of $Y$, such that

14.36
\begin{equation} \int _{U_ i}\frac{|F_ i|_{h_0}^2\mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )\Phi }}{R_ Y^ q(-\log R_ Y)^2}\, \mathrm dV<\infty \quad \text{for every }i, \end{equation}

and such that $G=\sum _ i\eta _ iF_ i$, each term extended by zero, is a smooth global section of $L$ with $G|_ Y=f$, $\bar\partial G|_ Y=0$, and

14.37
\begin{equation} \int _ X\frac{(|G|_{h_0}^2+|\bar\partial G|_{\omega ,h_0}^2) \mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )\Phi }}{R_ Y^ q(-\log R_ Y)^2}\, \mathrm dV<\infty . \end{equation}

In particular

14.38
\begin{equation} \int _{\{ R_ Y<\varepsilon \} }|\bar\partial G|_{\omega ,h}^2R_ Y^{-q}\, \mathrm dV \longrightarrow 0\qquad (\varepsilon \downarrow 0). \end{equation}

Here $\mathrm dV_ Y=\omega |_ Y^{n-q}/(n-q)!$; if $Y$ is a point, $\mathrm dV_ Y$ is the unit mass at that point.

Proof. In a holomorphic frame of $L$ the local weight $p$ of $h$ is plurisubharmonic, and its restriction to $Y$ is plurisubharmonic, because the restriction is defined and $Y$ is connected. If $Y$ is a point, the value of $p$ there is finite and we take any $\kappa >0$. Otherwise, by the strong openness theorem (corollary 12.5.4), every point of $Y$ has a neighborhood in $Y$ on which $f$ is square integrable with the weight $\mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )p|_ Y}$ for some $\kappa >0$; since $Y$ is compact, finitely many coordinate products $B'_ i\times B''_ i$ of the form of lemma 14.7.3, with tangential balls slightly larger than needed and each contained in a neighborhood on which (14.35) holds, cover $Y$, and one $\kappa >0$ works for all of them.

Lemma 14.7.3, applied in a frame with the plurisubharmonic weight $(1+\kappa )p$, gives holomorphic extensions $F_ i$ of $f$ on these products whose critical integrals with the logarithmic gain are finite. Let $U_ i$ be smaller concentric products that still cover $Y$. On $U_ i$, the smooth factors coming from the frames and the comparison $c|w|^2\leq R_ Y\leq C|w|^2$ of (14.35) preserve this finiteness, which gives (14.36); for the logarithmic factor we use that $-\log R_ Y\geq c'(-\log |w|^2)$ for a constant $c'>0$, since $R_ Y\leq C|w|^2$ and both $R_ Y$ and $|w|^2$ are less than $\mathrm e^{-4}$.

We now glue the local extensions. Choose smooth functions $\eta _ i$ with compact support in $U_ i$ whose sum is one on a neighborhood of $Y$; they are obtained by grouping the members of a partition of unity with compact supports subordinate to the cover of $X$ by the sets $U_ i$ and $X\setminus Y$ (proposition 7.1.3). Put $G=\sum _ i\eta _ iF_ i$, each term extended by zero. Then $G|_ Y=f$. Since all $F_ i$ are holomorphic with the same restriction $f$, we have $\bar\partial G=\sum _ i(\bar\partial \eta _ i)F_ i$, and on $Y$ this equals $f\, \bar\partial \sum _ i\eta _ i=0$. The bounds on the cutoffs and their derivatives and (14.36) give (14.37).

Put $\gamma =|\bar\partial G|_{\omega ,h_0}^2$. Since $\bar\partial G$ is smooth and vanishes on $Y$, we have $\gamma \leq C|w|^2$ in normal coordinates near each point of $Y$, hence $\gamma \leq CR_ Y$ near $Y$, by (14.35) and the compactness of $Y$. Let $A=\{ R_ Y<\varepsilon \} $; all integrals below are taken with $\mathrm dV$. Hölder's inequality with the exponents $1+\kappa $ and $(1+\kappa )/\kappa $ gives

\[ \int _ A\gamma \, \mathrm e^{-\Phi }R_ Y^{-q} \leq \left(\int _ A\frac{\gamma \mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )\Phi }}{R_ Y^ q(-\log R_ Y)^2}\right)^{1/(1+\kappa )} \left(\int _ A\gamma R_ Y^{-q}(-\log R_ Y)^{2/\kappa }\right)^{\kappa /(1+\kappa )}. \]

The first factor is bounded by (14.37). In normal polar coordinates, using (14.35) and $\gamma =O(R_ Y)$, the integrability of $\gamma R_ Y^{-q}(-\log R_ Y)^{2/\kappa }$ near $Y$ reduces to $\int _0^ r(-\log t)^{2/\kappa }\, \mathrm dt<\infty $. Hence the second factor tends to zero as $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$, by the absolute continuity of the integral, which proves (14.38). $\square$

Lemma 14.7.7. Let $L\to X$ be a holomorphic line bundle with a smooth quadratic metric $h_0$ and a plurisubharmonic metric $h=h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi }$, where $\Phi $ has gentle analytic singularities. Let $Y$ be a nonempty connected closed complex submanifold of codimension $q\geq 1$ to which $h$ restricts, let $f$ be a holomorphic section of $L|_ Y$ with $\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y<\infty $, and let $G$ be constructed from $f$ as in lemma 14.7.6. Then

14.39
\begin{equation} \varlimsup _{\varepsilon \downarrow 0} \int _{\{ \varepsilon /2\leq R_ Y\leq \varepsilon \} } |G|_ h^2R_ Y^{-q}\, \mathrm dV \leq C_ Y\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y, \qquad \mathrm dV_ Y=\omega |_ Y^{n-q}/(n-q)!, \end{equation}

where $C_ Y$ depends only on $Y$, $R_ Y$ and $\omega $, and not on $\Phi $ or $f$.

Proof. We compute on a log resolution adapted to $\Phi $ and $Y$; by lemma 14.7.5, only the strict transform of the exceptional divisor of the blowup of $Y$ contributes to the limit.

A resolution adapted to $\Phi $ and $Y$. Let $\operatorname {Bl}_ YX\to X$ be the blowup of $Y$, with exceptional divisor $E$; for $q=1$ this is the identity and $E=Y$. By proposition A.5.3, its Jacobian determinant is $\pm t^{q-1}$ in the charts of (A.8), where $E=\{ t=0\} $, so its relative canonical divisor is $(q-1)E$. Let $\mathfrak a$ be the ideal in the type of $\Phi $. We apply theorem A.5.5 on the compact manifold $\operatorname {Bl}_ YX$ to the pullback of $\mathfrak a$, which is nonzero, and to the divisor $E$. This gives a finite sequence of blowups with smooth centers, each having normal crossings with the accumulated divisor of theorem A.5.5, which starts with $E$; this accumulated divisor is the boundary in the sense of lemma 14.7.5. On the final model the boundary has simple normal crossings, and the pullback of $\mathfrak a$ is the ideal of an effective divisor supported in the boundary. Denote the composite map to $X$ by $\pi $. It is a composite of blowups along closed submanifolds that principalizes $\mathfrak a$, that is, a log resolution of $\mathfrak a$ in the sense of section 13.1. Since definition 13.1.11 requires a smooth remainder on every log resolution, $\Phi \circ \pi $ has a smooth remainder.

Divisor orders. In a normal-crossing chart on the final model write

\[ \Phi \circ \pi =\sum _ D a_ D\log |s_ D|^2+v,\qquad R_ Y\circ \pi =\prod _ D|s_ D|^{2b_ D}r, \]

with $v$ smooth, $r$ smooth and strictly positive, and $a_ D,b_ D\geq 0$. The Jacobian factor of the pulled-back volume is $\prod _ D|s_ D|^{2(A_ D-1)}$ times a smooth positive function, where $A_ D=A_ X(D)$ is the log discrepancy. Let $F_ i$ be one of the holomorphic extensions used in $G$, and suppose that the chart lies over the open set $U_ i$ of lemma 14.7.6. Then $F_ i\circ \pi $ vanishes to some integral order $e_{i,D}\geq 0$ along each coordinate divisor $D$ of the chart. By (14.36), the integral of $|F_ i|_{h_0}^2\mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )\Phi }R_ Y^{-q}(-\log R_ Y)^{-2}$ over $U_ i$ is finite, and this forces

14.40
\begin{equation} e_{i,D}+A_ D\geq (1+\kappa )a_ D+qb_ D\quad (b_ D>0), \qquad e_{i,D}+A_ D>(1+\kappa )a_ D\quad (b_ D=0). \end{equation}

Indeed, fix the other coordinates away from their divisors and integrate radially in $s_ D$: the integrand behaves like $|s_ D|^{2(e_{i,D}+A_ D-(1+\kappa )a_ D-qb_ D)-2}(-\log |s_ D|^2)^{-2}$ when $b_ D>0$, and the squared logarithm in the denominator makes the exponent $-1$ of the radial variable integrable but cannot compensate a smaller exponent. If $b_ D=0$, the logarithmic factor is bounded above and below near generic points of $D$, and the inequality must be strict. This is the computation of theorem 12.6.5 with one additional logarithmic factor.

Shrinking the chart, we may assume that for each $i$ either the chart lies over $U_ i$ or $\eta _ i\circ \pi $ vanishes on it, because $\operatorname{Supp}\eta _ i$ is a compact subset of the open set $U_ i$. Then, on a smaller chart, the pullback of $G=\sum _ i\eta _ iF_ i$ is the monomial $\prod _ Ds_ D^{e_ D}$, with $e_ D=\min _ ie_{i,D}$ over the indices $i$ of the first kind, times a smooth bounded function. The density of $|G|_ h^2R_ Y^{-q}\, \mathrm dV$ is therefore bounded by a constant times $\prod _ D|s_ D|^{2(\gamma _ D-1)}$, where

\[ \gamma _ D=e_ D-a_ D-qb_ D+A_ D. \]

If $a_ D>0$, (14.40) gives $\gamma _ D\geq \kappa a_ D>0$, and if $a_ D=b_ D=0$, it gives $\gamma _ D>0$. If $a_ D=0$ and $b_ D>0$, the formula for the relative canonical divisor of the first blowup gives $A_ D=A_{\operatorname {Bl}_ YX}(D)+(q-1)\operatorname {ord}_ D(E)$, and $b_ D=\operatorname {ord}_ D(E)$ because $\mathcal I_ Y$ pulls back to the ideal of $E$; hence

\[ A_ D-qb_ D=A_{\operatorname {Bl}_ YX}(D)-\operatorname {ord}_ D(E). \]

By lemma 14.7.5, applied with $Z=\operatorname {Bl}_ YX$, this is positive unless $D$ is the strict transform of $E$. For that divisor $b_ E=1$ and $A_ E=q$, and $a_ E=0$: since the restriction of $h$ to $Y$ is defined, generic points of $Y$ lie outside the pole set of $\Phi $. Hence $\gamma _ E=e_ E\geq 0$, and the strict transform of $E$ is the only divisor along which $\gamma _ D$ can vanish.

The annular limit. By a finite partition of unity on the compact inverse image of $Y$, it suffices to consider the integral over a small chart. If no coordinate divisor of the chart is the strict transform of $E$ with $\gamma _ E=0$, the density is integrable, the shells $\{ \varepsilon /2\leq R_ Y\leq \varepsilon \} $ shrink to the null set $\pi ^{-1}(Y)$, and dominated convergence shows that the integral over the shell tends to zero. Otherwise let $t$ be the coordinate defining the strict transform of $E$. The density is $|t|^{-2}$ times a smooth bounded coefficient and the factors $|s_ D|^{2(\gamma _ D-1)}$, $D\ne E$, which are integrable since $\gamma _ D>0$. For fixed values of the remaining coordinates $s$, the shell is $\varepsilon /2\leq |t|^2m(s)r(t,s)\leq \varepsilon $, with $m(s)=\prod _{D\ne E}|s_ D|^{2b_ D}$. Since $r$ is bounded above and below, the shell lies in a range of $\log |t|$ of bounded length, so the integral of $|t|^{-2}$ over it is bounded independently of $\varepsilon $ and $s$; this gives an integrable dominating function. As $\varepsilon \to 0$, the shell shrinks to $t=0$, the smooth coefficient tends to its value at $t=0$, and the integral of $|t|^{-2}$ over the shell tends to $\pi \log 2$. By dominated convergence, only the restriction to $t=0$ of the coefficient of $|t|^{-2}$ in the density contributes to the limit.

Identification of the limit. The argument just given, applied after multiplying the density by a continuous function with compact support, shows that the integrals over the shells converge to $\pi \log 2$ times the total mass of a measure $\mu $ on the strict transform of $E$; in a chart as above, $\mu $ has the density obtained by restricting to $t=0$ the smooth coefficient of $|t|^{-2}$, multiplied by the factors $|s_ D|^{2(\gamma _ D-1)}$, $D\ne E$. Since $\mu $ is determined by the limits of integrals over the shells $\{ \varepsilon /2\leq R_ Y\leq \varepsilon \} $, it does not depend on the coordinates in which it is computed, and since its density in every chart is integrable, it gives no mass to proper analytic subsets. We may therefore compute $\mu $ on the dense open subset of the strict transform of $E$ lying over the complement of the pole set of $\Phi $ in $Y$ and over which the further blowups are isomorphisms, using coordinates of the first blowup $\operatorname {Bl}_ YX$.

Let $(z',w)$ be coordinates on $X$ in which $Y=\{ w=0\} $, and write $\mathrm dV=J(z',w)\, \mathrm dV(z')\, \mathrm dV(w)$ with $J$ smooth and positive. By lemma 14.7.4, $R_ Y=Q_{z'}(w)+O(|w|^3)$ locally uniformly, where $Q_{z'}$ is a positive definite Hermitian form on $\mathbb C^ q$ depending continuously on $z'$. Over this chart, $\operatorname {Bl}_ YX$ is covered by charts with coordinates $(z',t,\xi )$, $\xi \in \mathbb C^{q-1}$, in which, after renumbering the coordinates $w$, we have $w=t(1,\xi )$ and $E=\{ t=0\} $ (for $q=1$ there is no $\xi $, and $t=w$). There $\mathrm dV(w)=|t|^{2(q-1)}\, \mathrm dV(t)\, \mathrm dV(\xi )$ and $R_ Y=|t|^2\bigl (Q_{z'}(1,\xi )+O(|t|)\bigr )$, so away from the poles of $\Phi $ the density of $|G|_ h^2R_ Y^{-q}\, \mathrm dV$ is $|t|^{-2}$ times the smooth function

\[ |G|_{h_0}^2\mathrm e^{-\Phi }\bigl (Q_{z'}(1,\xi )+O(|t|)\bigr )^{-q}J, \]

whose restriction to $t=0$ equals $|f(z')|_ h^2Q_{z'}(1,\xi )^{-q}J(z',0)$, because $G|_ Y=f$. The points of the projective normal fibers not covered by such a chart form a hyperplane in each fiber and are negligible. Integrating over $\xi $, we find that the image of $\mu $ under the projection to $Y$ has the density $c(z')|f(z')|_ h^2$ with respect to $\mathrm dV(z')$, where

\[ c(z')=J(z',0)\int _{\mathbb C^{q-1}}Q_{z'}(1,\xi )^{-q}\, \mathrm dV(\xi ). \]

Since $Q_{z'}(1,\xi )\geq c_0(1+|\xi |^2)$ with $c_0>0$ locally uniformly in $z'$, and $(1+|\xi |^2)^{-q}$ is integrable on $\mathbb C^{q-1}$, the function $c$ is locally bounded. It depends only on $\omega $, $R_ Y$ and the chart. Covering the compact manifold $Y$ by finitely many such charts and comparing $\mathrm dV(z')$ with $\mathrm dV_ Y$, we obtain (14.39). $\square$

Theorem 14.7.8. Let $Y\subset X$ be a nonempty closed connected complex submanifold, and put $\mathrm dV_ Y=\omega |_ Y^{\dim Y}/(\dim Y)!$, the unit mass if $Y$ is a point. There are constants $N,C>0$, depending only on $X$, $\omega $ and $Y$, with the following property. Let $L$ be a holomorphic line bundle with a smooth quadratic metric $h_0$ and a plurisubharmonic metric $h=h_0\mathrm e^{-\varphi }$ such that $c_1(L,h)\geq N\omega $, and suppose that the restriction of $h$ to $Y$ is defined, that is, the local weights of $h$ are not identically $-\infty $ on $Y$. Then every $f\in H^0(Y,L|_ Y)$ with $\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y<\infty $ has an extension $s\in H^0(X,L)$ such that

14.41
\begin{equation} s|_ Y=f,\qquad \int _ X|s|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV\leq C\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y. \end{equation}

Proof. If $Y=X$, take $s=f$, any $N$ and $C=1$. Suppose that $Y$ has codimension $q\geq 1$; if $f=0$, take $s=0$. As in the proof of theorem 14.6.7, we cut off a smooth extension of $f$ and correct the error, now with lemma 14.7.1; a pole of order $q$ along $Y$ in the weight makes the correction vanish on $Y$. Fix $R=R_ Y$ and the constant $b$ of lemma 14.7.4, with $\max _ XR<\mathrm e^{-4}$, and choose $b_ K\geq 0$ with $c_1(K_ X^{-1},h_{K^{-1},\omega })\geq -b_ K\omega $ (lemma 13.1.1). In Steps 1–4 we prove the theorem for a weight $\Phi $ with gentle analytic singularities, as for the approximations of theorem 13.4.5, under the curvature bound

\[ c_1(L,h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi })\geq N_0\omega , \qquad N_0=b_ K+(q+2)b+2. \]

In these steps $h$ denotes the metric $h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi }$, and $A$ the pole set of $\Phi $. Step 5 treats arbitrary weights.

Step 1: the twisted curvature form. For the given $f$ choose $G$ and $\kappa $ by lemma 14.7.6. On $M=X\setminus (A\cup Y)$ consider $F=L\otimes K_ X^{-1}$ with the metric $h\otimes h_{K^{-1},\omega }\mathrm e^{-q\psi }$, where $\psi =\log R$; the canonical identification of $L$-valued functions with $F$-valued $(n,0)$-forms preserves the squared norms times the volume. The manifold $M$ carries a Kähler form and cutoffs as required in lemma 14.7.1, by lemma 14.6.4 applied to $\Phi +\psi $, which is quasi-plurisubharmonic, has pole set $A\cup Y$ and is smooth on $M$. The resulting Kähler form depends on $\Phi $, but the estimate of lemma 14.7.1 does not involve it.

Let $\psi _\varepsilon =\log (\varepsilon +R)$, $\mu $, $a_\varepsilon $ and $b_\varepsilon $ be the functions of lemma 14.7.2, applied on $M$ to the smooth function $R$, which satisfies $0<R<\mathrm e^{-4}$ there, for $0<\varepsilon <\mathrm e^{-4}$. For fixed $\varepsilon $, $a_\varepsilon $ and $b_\varepsilon $ are bounded above and below by positive constants, $a_\varepsilon \geq 1$ and $1\leq -\mu '(\psi _\varepsilon )\leq 2$. By part (2) of that lemma, applied with $2\pi $ times the curvature form of the metric of $F$,

\[ H_\varepsilon =a_\varepsilon \bigl (2\pi c_1(L,h) +2\pi c_1(K_ X^{-1},h_{K^{-1},\omega }) +q\, \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \psi \bigr ) -\mu '(\psi _\varepsilon )\, \mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \psi _\varepsilon . \]

Since $\mathrm i\partial \bar\partial \psi =2\pi \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\psi \geq -2\pi b\omega $, $0\leq R/(\varepsilon +R)\leq 1$ and $-\mu '\leq 2$, the identity (14.33) gives

14.42
\begin{equation} H_\varepsilon \geq 2\pi \bigl (a_\varepsilon (N_0-b_ K-qb)-2b\bigr )\omega +\frac{\varepsilon }{R}\, \mathrm i\partial \psi _\varepsilon \wedge \bar\partial \psi _\varepsilon \geq 2\pi \omega +\frac{\varepsilon }{R}\, \mathrm i\partial \psi _\varepsilon \wedge \bar\partial \psi _\varepsilon , \end{equation}

where in the last step we used $a_\varepsilon \geq 1$ and $N_0-b_ K-qb=2b+2$. All constants in this display depend only on the geometry.

Step 2: the correction. Let $\rho \in C^\infty (\mathbb R)$ satisfy $0\leq \rho \leq 1$, $\rho =1$ on $(-\infty ,1/2]$ and $\rho =0$ on $[1,\infty )$, and put $G_\varepsilon =\rho (R/\varepsilon )G$. Since $\bar\partial R/\varepsilon =(1+R/\varepsilon )\bar\partial \psi _\varepsilon $, the $\bar\partial $-closed form $g_\varepsilon =\bar\partial G_\varepsilon $ splits as

\[ g_\varepsilon =g_\varepsilon ^{(1)}+g_\varepsilon ^{(2)},\qquad g_\varepsilon ^{(1)}=\rho '(R/\varepsilon )(1+R/\varepsilon ) \bar\partial \psi _\varepsilon \, G, \qquad g_\varepsilon ^{(2)}=\rho (R/\varepsilon )\bar\partial G, \]

regarded as $F$-valued $(n,1)$-forms. As in the proof of lemma 14.7.3, the rank-one part of (14.42) and lemma 14.6.1(1),(2) give

\[ |\langle g_\varepsilon ^{(1)},v\rangle |^2\leq 4\sup |\rho '|^2\, |G|^2\langle [H_\varepsilon ,\Lambda _\omega ]v,v\rangle \]

pointwise on the shell $\{ \varepsilon /2\leq R\leq \varepsilon \} $, where $R/\varepsilon \leq 1$, the norms being those of the metric of $F$. Hence the integral $M_{g_\varepsilon ^{(1)}}$ of lemma 14.7.1, formed with $H_\varepsilon $, is at most $C_1\int _{\{ \varepsilon /2\leq R\leq \varepsilon \} }|G|_ h^2R^{-q}\, \mathrm dV$, whose upper limit as $\varepsilon \to 0$ is at most $C_2\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y$ by lemma 14.7.7. Since $H_\varepsilon \geq 2\pi \omega $, lemma 14.6.1(3) bounds $M_{g_\varepsilon ^{(2)}}$ by

\[ C_3\int _{\{ R<\varepsilon \} }|\bar\partial G|_{\omega ,h}^2R^{-q}\, \mathrm dV, \]

which tends to zero by (14.38).

Both forms are square integrable for the metric of $F$. For $g_\varepsilon ^{(1)}$, the derivative of the cutoff is bounded for fixed $\varepsilon $, and (14.37) bounds the integral of $|G|_ h^2R^{-q}$ over the shell, where the logarithm is bounded and $\mathrm e^{-\Phi }\leq \mathrm e^{\kappa \sup _ X\Phi }\mathrm e^{-(1+\kappa )\Phi }$; for $g_\varepsilon ^{(2)}$ this follows from (14.38). Since $M_{g'+g''}\leq 2(M_{g'}+M_{g''})$, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the positive form $\langle [H_\varepsilon ,\Lambda _\omega ]^{-1}\cdot ,\cdot \rangle $, lemma 14.7.1 gives $u_\varepsilon $ on $M$ with

14.43
\begin{equation} \bar\partial u_\varepsilon =g_\varepsilon ,\qquad \varlimsup _{\varepsilon \downarrow 0} \int _ M\frac{|u_\varepsilon |_ h^2R^{-q}}{a_\varepsilon +b_\varepsilon }\, \mathrm dV \leq C_4\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y. \end{equation}

The constants $C_1,\ldots ,C_4$ depend only on the geometry and the fixed cutoff $\rho $; neither the exponent $\kappa $ nor the derivatives of the local extensions $F_ i$ enter them.

Step 3: holomorphic sections with the trace $f$. For fixed $\varepsilon $ the denominator $a_\varepsilon +b_\varepsilon $ is bounded, and $h\geq \mathrm e^{-\sup _ X\Phi }h_0$ because $\Phi $ is bounded above on the compact manifold $X$. Hence $s_\varepsilon =G_\varepsilon -u_\varepsilon $, which is holomorphic on $M$, is square integrable for $h_0$, and it extends to a global holomorphic section by lemma 14.6.6. Then $u_\varepsilon =G_\varepsilon -s_\varepsilon $ is smooth on $X$.

It remains to show that $s_\varepsilon |_ Y=f$. Let $y\in Y\setminus A$, and choose holomorphic coordinates $(z',w)$ near $y$ with $Y=\{ w=0\} $. Near $y$ the weight $\Phi $ is smooth and $a_\varepsilon +b_\varepsilon $ is bounded, so $\int |u_\varepsilon |_{h_0}^2R^{-q}\, \mathrm dV$ is finite over a neighborhood of $y$. Since $R\leq C|w|^2$ near $y$ by lemma 14.7.4, and $\int _{|w|<r}|w|^{-2q}\, \mathrm dV(w)=\infty $ in $\mathbb C^ q$ for every $r>0$, by polar coordinates, the continuous function $u_\varepsilon $ vanishes at $y$. Hence $u_\varepsilon =0$ on $Y\setminus A$, and $s_\varepsilon =G=f$ there. Since the restriction of $h$ to $Y$ is defined, $A\cap Y$ is a proper analytic subset of the connected manifold $Y$, and the identity theorem gives $s_\varepsilon |_ Y=f$.

Step 4: the norm bound. For all sufficiently small $\varepsilon $,

\[ (a_\varepsilon +b_\varepsilon )R^ q \leq C_5 R^ q(-\log R)^2\leq C_6, \]

because $a_\varepsilon +b_\varepsilon \leq 5\psi ^2$ by lemma 14.7.2(1), and because $R^ q(-\log R)^2$ is bounded on $X$. Consequently (14.43) bounds $\int _ X|u_\varepsilon |_ h^2\, \mathrm dV$ by $C_6$ times the integral there. The section $G$ has finite $h$-norm, since (14.37) controls it, $\Phi $ being bounded above and $R^ q(-\log R)^2$ bounded; therefore $\| G_\varepsilon \| _ h\to 0$ as $\varepsilon \to 0$, by absolute continuity of the integral on the shrinking supports. The triangle inequality gives

\[ \varlimsup _{\varepsilon \downarrow 0}\| s_\varepsilon \| _ h^2 \leq C_7\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y, \]

with $C_7=C_6C_4$. The sections $s_\varepsilon $ are bounded in $L^2$ for $h_0$, so, by lemma 14.5.2(2), a sequence $\varepsilon _ k\to 0$ gives sections converging uniformly to a holomorphic section $s$. Its restriction to $Y$ is $f$, and Fatou's lemma gives $\| s\| _ h^2\leq C_7\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y$. This proves the theorem for gentle weights with the constant $C_7$, which depends only on the geometry.

Step 5: arbitrary weights. Put $N=N_0+1$. By theorem 13.4.5, applied to $\varphi $ with the background $c_1(L,h_0)-N\omega $, there are $\Phi _ j\downarrow \varphi $ with gentle analytic singularities of the kind required in the gentle case and $c_1(L,h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi _ j})\geq (N-\delta _ j)\omega $, where $\delta _ j\to 0$; for large $j$ the curvature is at least $N_0\omega $. Since $\Phi _ j\geq \varphi $, the restrictions of the metrics $h_0\mathrm e^{-\Phi _ j}$ to $Y$ are defined, and the norms of $f$ for them are at most its norm for $h$. The gentle case gives $s_ j\in H^0(X,L)$ with $s_ j|_ Y=f$ and $\int _ X|s_ j|_{h_0}^2\mathrm e^{-\Phi _ j}\, \mathrm dV\leq C_7\int _ Y|f|_ h^2\, \mathrm dV_ Y$. Since $\Phi _ j\leq \Phi _1$, the $s_ j$ are bounded in $L^2$ for $h_0$, and by lemma 14.5.2(2) a subsequence converges uniformly to a holomorphic section $s$ with $s|_ Y=f$. The weights $\mathrm e^{-\Phi _ j}$ converge to $\mathrm e^{-\varphi }$ wherever $\varphi $ is finite, hence almost everywhere, and Fatou's lemma gives (14.41) with $C=C_7$. $\square$

Corollary 14.7.9. Let $L$ and $T$ be holomorphic line bundles on $X$ with smooth quadratic metrics $h_0$ and $h_ T$, put $\theta =c_1(L,h_0)$, and choose $b_ T\geq 0$ with $c_1(T,h_ T)\geq -b_ T\omega $. Let $\varphi $ be quasi-plurisubharmonic with $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \omega $ for some $\delta >0$, and let $Y\subset X$ be a nonempty closed connected complex submanifold such that $\varphi |_ Y$ is not identically $-\infty $. Let $N$ and $C$ be the constants of theorem 14.7.8. Then for every integer $k\geq 1$ with $k\delta -b_ T\geq N$, every holomorphic section $f$ of $(T\otimes L^ k)|_ Y$ with finite norm for $h_ T\otimes h_0^ k\mathrm e^{-k\varphi |_ Y}$ extends to a holomorphic section $s$ of $T\otimes L^ k$ on $X$ with

\[ \int _ X|s|_{h_ T\otimes h_0^ k}^2\mathrm e^{-k\varphi }\, \mathrm dV \leq C\int _ Y|f|_{h_ T\otimes h_0^ k}^2 \mathrm e^{-k\varphi |_ Y}\, \mathrm dV_ Y. \]

Proof. The metric $h_ T\otimes h_0^ k\mathrm e^{-k\varphi }$ on $T\otimes L^ k$ is plurisubharmonic, its curvature current is $c_1(T,h_ T)+k(\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi )\geq (k\delta -b_ T)\omega \geq N\omega $, and its restriction to $Y$ is defined. Hence theorem 14.7.8 applies. Note that the finiteness of the norm on $Y$ means that $f$ lies locally in the multiplier ideal of $k\varphi |_ Y$, and that the constant $C$ is multiplied by $n!/(\dim Y)!$ if the volumes $\omega ^ n$ and $\omega |_ Y^{\dim Y}$ are used instead of $\mathrm dV$ and $\mathrm dV_ Y$. $\square$

14.7.1 Extending strictly positive potentials

We keep the compact Kähler manifold $(X,\omega )$, but now we extend potentials instead of sections. Let $Y\subset X$ be a closed complex submanifold, and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\omega |_ Y)$ be such that $\omega |_ Y+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi $ is a Kähler current on $Y$, that is, dominates a positive multiple of $\omega |_ Y$. We look for $\widetilde\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ that restricts exactly to $\varphi $ and for which $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde\varphi $ is again a Kähler current (theorem 14.7.12).

Near $Y$, local extensions are obtained by pulling $\varphi $ back by a holomorphic projection onto $Y$ and adding a large multiple of a function vanishing to second order along $Y$. The difficulty is to glue them, since two projections give different pullbacks. We take a maximum after lowering each local extension by a cutoff penalty near the edge of its chart. This maximum is $\omega $-plurisubharmonic near $Y$ and keeps the prescribed values on $Y$, provided that near every point of $Y$ on the edge of a chart the penalized extension from that chart is dominated by the extension from another chart. To obtain this comparison, we add to $\mathrm e^\varphi $ the exponential of a small logarithmic pole along $Y$: when $\mathrm e^\varphi $ is Hölder continuous, the difference between two pullbacks of $\mathrm e^\varphi $ is small compared with this exponential near $Y$. The logarithm of such a sum is again quasi-plurisubharmonic (lemma 14.7.11), and the Hölder continuity survives the operations of the construction (lemma 14.7.10).

Hölder continuity on a manifold is defined in charts. Let $M$ be a smooth manifold. A function $f\colon M\to \mathbb R$ is Hölder continuous if every point of $M$ has a coordinate neighborhood $V$, with coordinates $x\colon V\to \mathbb R^ d$, on which there are constants $C\geq 0$ and $\alpha \in (0,1]$ such that

\[ |f(a)-f(b)|\leq C|x(a)-x(b)|^\alpha \qquad \text{for all }a,b\in V. \]

We then say that $f$ is Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $ on $V$, in the coordinates $x$. Such a function is continuous on $V$, and hence bounded near each point of $V$.

Lemma 14.7.10. Let $M$ and $M'$ be smooth manifolds.

  1. Let $f$ be Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $ on a coordinate neighborhood $V$ in the coordinates $x$, let $p\in V$, and let $y$ be coordinates on a neighborhood of $p$. Then there is a neighborhood of $p$ on which $f$ is Hölder continuous in the coordinates $y$ with exponent $\alpha '$ for every $\alpha '\in (0,\alpha ]$.

  2. If $M$ is compact and $f$ is Hölder continuous on $M$, then there are $\alpha \in (0,1]$ and finitely many coordinate neighborhoods covering $M$ on each of which $f$ is Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $.

  3. Smooth functions are Hölder continuous. If $f$ and $g$ are Hölder continuous on $M$, then so are $f+g$, $fg$ and $\max \{ f,g\} $. If $f\geq 0$ is Hölder continuous and $0<\gamma \leq 1$, then $f^\gamma $ is Hölder continuous.

  4. If $\Phi \colon M'\to M$ is a smooth map and $f$ is Hölder continuous on $M$, then $f\circ \Phi $ is Hölder continuous on $M'$.

Proof. (1) Choose a closed ball $N$ in the $y$-coordinates, centered at $y(p)$, whose preimage lies in $V$. The map $x\circ y^{-1}$ is smooth on the convex compact set $N$, so by the mean value inequality it is Lipschitz on $N$ with some constant $L$. Moreover $N$ has some diameter $D$, and $t^\alpha \leq D^{\alpha -\alpha '}t^{\alpha '}$ for $0\leq t\leq D$. Hence, for $a,b$ in the preimage of $N$, which is a neighborhood of $p$,

\begin{align*} |f(a)-f(b)|& \leq C|x(a)-x(b)|^\alpha \leq CL^\alpha |y(a)-y(b)|^\alpha \\ & \leq CL^\alpha D^{\alpha -\alpha '}|y(a)-y(b)|^{\alpha '}. \end{align*}

(2) By (1), applied with $y=x$, every point of $M$ has a coordinate neighborhood on which $f$ is Hölder continuous with some exponent, and on which it remains so with every smaller exponent. Finitely many of these neighborhoods cover $M$, and the smallest of their exponents serves on all of them.

(3) A smooth function is Lipschitz on every closed coordinate ball, by the mean value inequality, so it is Hölder continuous with exponent one. Let $f$ and $g$ be Hölder continuous and $p\in M$. By (1) there are coordinates $x$ on a neighborhood $V$ of $p$ and $\alpha \in (0,1]$ such that $f$ and $g$ are Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $ on $V$ in these coordinates, say with the constant $C$; shrinking $V$, we may assume that $|f|,|g|\leq B$ on $V$. For $a,b\in V$ we have $|(f+g)(a)-(f+g)(b)|\leq 2C|x(a)-x(b)|^\alpha $,

\begin{align*} |f(a)g(a)-f(b)g(b)|& \leq |f(a)|\, |g(a)-g(b)|+|g(b)|\, |f(a)-f(b)|\\ & \leq 2BC|x(a)-x(b)|^\alpha , \end{align*}

and $|\max \{ f,g\} (a)-\max \{ f,g\} (b)|\leq \max \{ |f(a)-f(b)|,|g(a)-g(b)|\} $.

For the power, note first that $(s+t)^\gamma \leq s^\gamma +t^\gamma $ for $s,t\geq 0$: if $s+t>0$, divide by $(s+t)^\gamma $ and use $\lambda ^\gamma \geq \lambda $ for $\lambda \in [0,1]$. Hence, if $s\geq t\geq 0$, then $s^\gamma \leq (s-t)^\gamma +t^\gamma $, that is, $|s^\gamma -t^\gamma |\leq |s-t|^\gamma $ for all $s,t\geq 0$. If $f\geq 0$ has exponent $\alpha $ on $V$ with the constant $C$, it follows that $|f(a)^\gamma -f(b)^\gamma |\leq C^\gamma |x(a)-x(b)|^{\alpha \gamma }$.

(4) Let $p'\in M'$, let $V$ be a coordinate neighborhood of $\Phi (p')$, with coordinates $x$, on which $f$ is Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $ and constant $C$, and let $N$ be a closed coordinate ball around $p'$, in coordinates $x'$, with $\Phi (N)\subset V$. The map $x\circ \Phi $, written in the coordinates $x'$, is smooth on the convex compact set $N$, hence Lipschitz on $N$ with some constant $L$. Therefore $|f(\Phi (a))-f(\Phi (b))|\leq CL^\alpha |x'(a)-x'(b)|^\alpha $ for $a,b\in N$. $\square$

Lemma 14.7.11. Let $U$ be an open subset of a complex manifold, let $\theta $ be a smooth closed real $(1,1)$-form on $U$, and let $a,b\in \mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta )$. Then

\[ \log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)\in \mathrm{PSH}(U,\theta ), \]

where $\mathrm e^{-\infty }=0$ and $\log 0=-\infty $, so that this function equals $-\infty $ precisely at the common poles of $a$ and $b$. Moreover

\[ \max \{ a,b\} \leq \log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)\leq \max \{ a,b\} +\log 2. \]

Proof. We begin with the inequalities, which are pointwise. Fix a point and suppose that $a\geq b$ there; the other case is symmetric. If $a=-\infty $, then $b=-\infty $ as well, and all three expressions equal $-\infty $. Otherwise $\log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)=a+\log (1+\mathrm e^{b-a})$, and $0\leq \log (1+\mathrm e^{b-a})\leq \log 2$.

Put $H(x,y)=\log (\mathrm e^ x+\mathrm e^ y)$ for $x,y\in [-\infty ,\infty )$, with the same conventions. On $\mathbb R^2$ the function $H$ is smooth and increasing in each variable: its partial derivatives are $p$ and $1-p$, where $p=\mathrm e^ x/(\mathrm e^ x+\mathrm e^ y)$, and its Hessian matrix is $p(1-p)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1& -1\\ -1& 1\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. On $[-\infty ,\infty )^2$ it is still increasing in each variable, and it is continuous, with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$. Indeed, by the inequalities just proved, $\max \{ x,y\} \leq H(x,y)\leq \max \{ x,y\} +\log 2$, so $H(x,y)\to -\infty $ when $\max \{ x,y\} \to -\infty $; and $H(x,y)=y+\log (1+\mathrm e^{x-y})\to y_0$ when $x\to -\infty $ and $y\to y_0\in \mathbb R$.

The first assertion is local. Let $B\Subset U$ be a coordinate ball, let $s$ be a smooth real function on $B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s=\theta $ (lemma 13.1.2), and let $B'\Subset B$ be a smaller concentric ball. Then $a+s$ and $b+s$ are plurisubharmonic on $B$, by lemma 13.1.4(2), and $\log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)+s=H(a+s,b+s)$. If $f,g$ are smooth plurisubharmonic functions, the chain rule gives

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H(f,g)=p\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f+(1-p)\, \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g +p(1-p)\, \frac{\mathrm i}{2\pi }\, \partial (f-g)\wedge \bar\partial (f-g)\geq 0, \]

with $p$ evaluated at $(f,g)$; hence $H(f,g)$ is plurisubharmonic, by proposition 8.2.2(1). In general, let $f_ k$ and $g_ k$ be the regularizations of $a+s$ and $b+s$ by radial mollifiers whose radii decrease to zero and are smaller than the distance from $B'$ to $\partial B$. By proposition 8.2.2(2), they are smooth plurisubharmonic functions on $B'$, and they decrease to $a+s$ and $b+s$. Since $H$ is continuous and increasing in each variable on $[-\infty ,\infty )^2$, the functions $H(f_ k,g_ k)$ decrease to $H(a+s,b+s)$. This limit is at least $a+s$, so it is not identically $-\infty $, and it is plurisubharmonic on $B'$ by theorem 8.3.1. Consequently $\log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)=H(a+s,b+s)-s$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $B'$ and $\theta +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log (\mathrm e^ a+\mathrm e^ b)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}H(a+s,b+s)\geq 0$ there. Every point of $U$ is the center of such a ball $B'$, so the first assertion follows. $\square$

Theorem 14.7.12. Let $Y\subset X$ be a closed complex submanifold, and let $\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(Y,\omega |_ Y)$ satisfy

\[ \omega |_ Y+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq \delta \, \omega |_ Y \]

for some $\delta >0$. Suppose that $\mathrm e^\varphi $, defined to be zero at the poles of $\varphi $, is Hölder continuous on $Y$. Then there are $\widetilde\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ and $\varepsilon >0$ such that

\[ \widetilde\varphi |_ Y=\varphi ,\qquad \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde\varphi \geq \varepsilon \omega . \]

Moreover $\widetilde\varphi $ is continuous on $X\setminus Y$, and $\mathrm e^{\widetilde\varphi }$ is Hölder continuous on $X$. If $\varphi $ has analytic singularities, then so does $\widetilde\varphi $.

Proof. We construct local extensions near $Y$ (Steps 1 and 2), compare them on overlaps (Step 3), glue them (Step 4), extend the result from a neighborhood of $Y$ to $X$ (Step 5), and finally treat analytic singularities (Step 6).

Step 0. Reductions. If $Y=\varnothing $, the function $\widetilde\varphi =0$ and $\varepsilon =1$ have all the stated properties; $0=\log |1|^2$ has analytic singularities. Let $Y\neq \varnothing $. The connected components of $Y$ are open in the compact set $Y$, so there are finitely many of them, say $Y_1,\ldots ,Y_ r$; each $Y_ k$ is closed in $X$, hence a compact connected complex submanifold, and different components may have different dimensions. If some $Y_ k$ has dimension $n$, then it is open in $X$, because near each of its points it is a coordinate plane of full dimension; being also closed, it equals $X$, as $X$ is connected. Then $Y=X$, and $\widetilde\varphi =\varphi $ with $\varepsilon =\delta $ has the stated properties. We may therefore assume that every component of $Y$ has positive codimension; components of dimension zero are allowed.

Fix $\varepsilon $ with $0<\varepsilon <\min \{ \delta /4,1/4\} $. By lemma 14.7.10(2), $\mathrm e^\varphi $ is Hölder continuous with one exponent $\alpha \in (0,1]$ on finitely many coordinate neighborhoods covering $Y$. By lemma 14.7.10(1), every point $p\in Y$ then has, in every system of coordinates of $Y$ near $p$, a neighborhood on which $\mathrm e^\varphi $ is Hölder continuous with exponent $\alpha $.

Step 1. A small normal pole. For each $k$ let $R_ k=R_{Y_ k}$ be the function of lemma 14.7.4 for the submanifold $Y_ k$, and let $\kappa _ k\geq 0$ be the constant called $b$ there, so that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log R_ k\geq -\kappa _ k\omega $. Put

\[ R=R_1\cdots R_ r,\qquad \kappa =\kappa _1+\cdots +\kappa _ r. \]

Then $R\geq 0$ is smooth and vanishes exactly on $Y$. The function $\log R=\sum _ k\log R_ k$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\log R\geq -\kappa \omega $, and has analytic singularities by proposition 13.1.7, as a sum of functions with analytic singularities. Choose a rational number $c$ with $0<c<\alpha $ and $c\kappa /2\leq 1-4\varepsilon $; this is possible because $1-4\varepsilon >0$. Put $F=(c/2)\log R$. Then

\[ \omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F\geq \Bigl (1-\frac{c\kappa }2\Bigr )\omega \geq 4\varepsilon \omega . \]

The function $F$ is smooth on $X\setminus Y$, equals $-\infty $ on $Y$, and has analytic singularities, since $c/2$ is rational. Its exponential $\mathrm e^ F=R^{c/2}$ is Hölder continuous on $X$ by lemma 14.7.10(3).

We need a lower bound for $R$ near $Y$. Let $p\in Y_ k$, and let $(z',w)$ be holomorphic coordinates near $p$ in which $Y=\{ w=0\} $. The function $P=\prod _{l\neq k}R_ l$ is smooth and positive near $p$, since the closed sets $Y_ l$, $l\neq k$, do not contain $p$. On a neighborhood of $p$ that does not meet these sets we have $Y_ k=Y=\{ w=0\} $, so the second inequality in (14.35) of lemma 14.7.4, applied to $Y_ k$, bounds $R_ k$ below by a positive multiple of $|w|^2$ near $p$. Since $R=R_ kP$ and $P$ is bounded away from zero near $p$, there are a closed ball $K$ centered at $p$ and a constant $c_0>0$ such that

14.44
\begin{equation} R\geq c_0|w|^2,\qquad \mathrm e^ F=R^{c/2}\geq c_0^{c/2}|w|^ c\qquad \text{on }K. \end{equation}

Moreover, at the points $(z',0)$ of the interior of $K$, the real Hessian $D_ w^2R(z',0)$ of $w\mapsto R(z',w)$ at $w=0$ is positive definite. Indeed, $R\geq 0$ attains its minimum $0$ at such a point, so its first derivatives vanish there, and Taylor's formula gives $R(z',t\xi )=\frac{t^2}2D_ w^2R(z',0)[\xi ,\xi ]+O(t^3)$ as $t\to 0$, for every $\xi $; comparing with $R(z',t\xi )\geq c_0t^2|\xi |^2$ for small $t$, we obtain $D_ w^2R(z',0)[\xi ,\xi ]\geq 2c_0|\xi |^2$.

Step 2. Local extensions. Every point of $Y$ has holomorphic coordinates $(z',w)\in \mathbb C^ m\times \mathbb C^{n-m}$, centered at that point, in which $Y=\{ w=0\} $ on the coordinate domain, where $m<n$ is the dimension of the component of $Y$ through the point. A product of two small balls centered at the origin is then a chart $\widehat W=\widehat B'\times \widehat B''$ with $Y\cap \widehat W=\widehat B'\times \{ 0\} $. The products $B'\times B''$ of the concentric balls of half the radii cover $Y$, and since $Y$ is compact, finitely many of them suffice. We thus obtain finitely many charts $W_ j=B'_ j\times B''_ j$, relatively compact in charts $\widehat W_ j=\widehat B'_ j\times \widehat B''_ j$ of the same form, with $Y\cap W_ j=B'_ j\times \{ 0\} $ and with dimensions $m_ j<n$. Let $\pi _ j\colon \widehat W_ j\to Y$, $\pi _ j(z',w)=(z',0)$, be the holomorphic projection.

Below we shrink the normal factors $B''_ j$ several times, keeping them balls centered at the origin; this does not change $Y\cap W_ j$, so the charts keep covering $Y$. If a property holds on an open set $O\subset \widehat W_ j$ containing the compact set $\overline{B'_ j}\times \{ 0\} $, then this compact set has positive distance from the complement of $O$ in the coordinates, so $O$ contains $W_ j$ after the normal factor has been shrunk. We claim that there is $A>0$ such that, after such a shrinking,

\[ a_ j=\varphi \circ \pi _ j+AR,\qquad b_ j=F+AR \]

belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(W_ j,(1-2\varepsilon )\omega )$ for every $j$.

Since $4\varepsilon \leq \delta $, the hypothesis gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi \geq -(1-\delta )\omega |_ Y\geq -(1-4\varepsilon )\omega |_ Y$. Choose a ball $\widetilde B'_ j$, concentric with $B'_ j$, with $\overline{B'_ j}\subset \widetilde B'_ j\Subset \widehat B'_ j$, and a smooth function $s$ on $\widetilde B'_ j\times \{ 0\} $ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s=(1-4\varepsilon )\omega |_ Y$ there (lemma 13.1.2; if $m_ j=0$, take $s=0$). Then $\varphi +s$ is plurisubharmonic on $\widetilde B'_ j\times \{ 0\} $, by lemma 13.1.4(2). By proposition 8.2.3, $(\varphi +s)\circ \pi _ j$ is plurisubharmonic or identically $-\infty $ on the connected set $\widetilde B'_ j\times \widehat B''_ j$, and the second alternative is excluded because $\pi _ j$ maps this set onto $\widetilde B'_ j\times \{ 0\} $, where $\varphi +s$ is not identically $-\infty $. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(s\circ \pi _ j)=\pi _ j^*\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}s$ for the smooth function $s$, we obtain that $\varphi \circ \pi _ j$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $\widetilde B'_ j\times \widehat B''_ j\supset \overline{W_ j}$ and

\[ \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(\varphi \circ \pi _ j)\geq -(1-4\varepsilon )\pi _ j^*(\omega |_ Y). \]

For $a_ j$ it therefore suffices that the smooth form

\[ Q_ j=(1-2\varepsilon )\omega -(1-4\varepsilon )\pi _ j^*(\omega |_ Y)+A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R \]

be positive on $W_ j$.

We first examine $Q_ j$ at the points of $Y$. Write a real $(1,1)$-form on $W_ j$ as $\mathrm i\sum _{\mu ,\nu }g_{\mu \nu }\, \mathrm d\zeta _\mu \wedge \mathrm d\bar\zeta _\nu $, where $\zeta =(z',w)$, and split its Hermitian coefficient matrix $(g_{\mu \nu })$ into blocks with respect to the tangential coordinates $z'$ and the normal coordinates $w$; the form is positive definite at a point if and only if this matrix is. The form $\pi _ j^*(\omega |_ Y)$ involves only $\mathrm dz'$ and $\mathrm d\bar z'$, and at a point $(z',0)$ its coefficients are those of $\omega |_ Y$ at that point. Hence its mixed and normal blocks vanish, and at points of $Y$ its tangential block is the tangential block $\Omega _ T$ of $\omega $.

The function $R$ vanishes on $Y$ together with its first derivatives. Differentiating the identities $R(z',0)=0$ and $\partial R/\partial \bar w_\nu (z',0)=0$ with respect to $z'$ and $\bar z'$ shows that $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R$ has zero tangential and mixed blocks along $Y$. Its normal block is $(2\pi )^{-1}$ times the matrix $N_ R=(\partial ^2R/\partial w_\mu \partial \bar w_\nu )$, which is positive definite at points of $Y$: for $\xi \in \mathbb C^{n-m_ j}\setminus \{ 0\} $, applying the identity $\Delta =4\partial _ t\partial _{\bar t}$ to $t\mapsto R(z',t\xi )$ at $t=0$ gives

\[ \sum _{\mu ,\nu }\frac{\partial ^2R}{\partial w_\mu \partial \bar w_\nu }(z',0)\xi _\mu \bar\xi _\nu =\frac14\bigl (D_ w^2R(z',0)[\xi ,\xi ]+D_ w^2R(z',0)[\mathrm i\xi ,\mathrm i\xi ]\bigr )>0 \]

by Step 1.

Consequently, at a point of $Y$, the tangential block of $Q_ j$ is $2\varepsilon \Omega _ T$, which is positive definite, its mixed block is the mixed block $\Omega _ M$ of $(1-2\varepsilon )\omega $, and its normal block is $\Omega _ N+AN_ R/(2\pi )$, where $\Omega _ N$ is the normal block of $(1-2\varepsilon )\omega $. By the Schur complement criterion for Hermitian block matrices whose upper left block is positive definite, $Q_ j$ is positive definite at a point of $Y$ if and only if

\[ \Omega _ N+\frac A{2\pi }N_ R-\Omega _ M^*\bigl (2\varepsilon \, \Omega _ T\bigr )^{-1}\Omega _ M \]

is positive definite there. (If $m_ j=0$, there is no tangential block, and the condition reads $\Omega _ N+AN_ R/(2\pi )>0$.) All blocks depend continuously on the point, $N_ R$ is positive definite, and the sets $\overline{B'_ j}\times \{ 0\} $ are compact and finite in number. Hence the smallest eigenvalue of $N_ R$ on them has a positive lower bound and the other two terms are bounded, so one $A$ makes this matrix positive definite at every point of every $\overline{B'_ j}\times \{ 0\} $. Fix such an $A$. Since the coefficients of $Q_ j$ are continuous on $\widehat W_ j$ and positive definiteness is an open condition, $Q_ j$ is positive definite on an open set containing $\overline{B'_ j}\times \{ 0\} $, and we shrink the normal factor $B''_ j$ so that $W_ j$ lies in this set. This proves the claim for $a_ j$.

Along $Y$ the form $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R$ is positive semidefinite, so $A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R+\varepsilon \omega $ is positive definite on an open set containing $\overline{B'_ j}\times \{ 0\} $; after shrinking the normal factors once more we have $A\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R\geq -\varepsilon \omega $ on every $W_ j$. Together with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}F\geq -(1-4\varepsilon )\omega $ this gives

\[ (1-2\varepsilon )\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}b_ j\geq (1-2\varepsilon )\omega -(1-4\varepsilon )\omega -\varepsilon \omega =\varepsilon \omega \geq 0, \]

which proves the claim for $b_ j$.

By lemma 14.7.11, applied with $\theta =(1-2\varepsilon )\omega $, the functions

\[ \varphi _ j=\log (\mathrm e^{a_ j}+\mathrm e^{b_ j}) =\log (\mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ j}+\mathrm e^ F)+AR \]

belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(W_ j,(1-2\varepsilon )\omega )$. On $Y$ we have $F=-\infty $, $R=0$ and $\varphi \circ \pi _ j=\varphi $, so $\varphi _ j=\varphi $ on $Y\cap W_ j$, also at the poles of $\varphi $. Off $Y$ the function $\mathrm e^ F$ is positive and continuous and $\mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ j}$ is continuous, so $\varphi _ j$ is continuous on $W_ j\setminus Y$. Finally $\mathrm e^{\varphi _ j}=(\mathrm e^\varphi \circ \pi _ j+\mathrm e^ F)\, \mathrm e^{AR}$ is Hölder continuous on $W_ j$ by lemma 14.7.10(3),(4).

Step 3. Comparison on overlaps. We claim that for every $p\in Y\cap W_ i\cap W_ j$ and every $\eta >0$ there is a neighborhood of $p$ on which

14.45
\begin{equation} \varphi _ i\leq \varphi _ j+\eta . \end{equation}

Work in the coordinates $(z',w)$ of $W_ i$, and write $p=(z'_ p,0)$. The coordinate $z'$ restricts to coordinates of $Y$ on $Y\cap W_ i$, so by Step 0 there are a neighborhood $V_ p$ of $p$ in $Y\cap W_ i$ and a constant $C_ H$ with $|\mathrm e^{\varphi (y_1)}-\mathrm e^{\varphi (y_2)}|\leq C_ H|z'(y_1)-z'(y_2)|^\alpha $ for $y_1,y_2\in V_ p$. Since $\pi _ i$ and $\pi _ j$ are continuous and fix $p$, there is a closed ball $K\subset W_ i\cap W_ j$ centered at $p$ such that $\pi _ i(K)\cup \pi _ j(K)\subset V_ p$ and (14.44) holds on $K$ in these coordinates. The map $z'\circ \pi _ j$ is smooth on the convex compact set $K$, so it is Lipschitz there with some constant $L$. Let $x=(z',w)\in K$, and put $y=(z',0)=\pi _ i(x)$. Then $|y-p|\leq |x-p|$, so $y\in K\cap Y$, and $\pi _ j(y)=y$ because $\pi _ j$ is the identity on $Y\cap W_ j$. Hence

\[ |z'(\pi _ i(x))-z'(\pi _ j(x))|=|z'(\pi _ j(y))-z'(\pi _ j(x))|\leq L|y-x|=L|w|, \]

and therefore

\[ \bigl |\mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ i}(x)-\mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ j}(x)\bigr |\leq C_ HL^\alpha |w|^\alpha . \]

Since $c<\alpha $, we have $C_ HL^\alpha |w|^\alpha \leq (\mathrm e^\eta -1)c_0^{c/2}|w|^ c$ whenever $|w|$ is sufficiently small, and then (14.44) gives $C_ HL^\alpha |w|^\alpha \leq (\mathrm e^\eta -1)\mathrm e^{F(x)}$. At such points $x\in K$, including the points of $Y$,

\[ \mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ i}+\mathrm e^ F \leq \mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ j}+\mathrm e^\eta \mathrm e^ F \leq \mathrm e^\eta \bigl (\mathrm e^{\varphi \circ \pi _ j}+\mathrm e^ F\bigr ). \]

The logarithm is increasing on $[0,\infty )$, with $\log 0=-\infty $, so taking logarithms and adding $AR$ to both sides gives (14.45) on the neighborhood of $p$ formed by the interior points $x$ of $K$ with $|w(x)|$ sufficiently small.

Step 4. Gluing near $Y$. Every point of $Y$ has an open neighborhood whose closure is contained in some $W_ j$, and finitely many of these neighborhoods cover $Y$; let $W'_ j$ be the union of those chosen inside $W_ j$. Then $W'_ j\Subset W_ j$ and the $W'_ j$ cover $Y$. Choose open sets $U_ j$ and $T_ j$ with $W'_ j\Subset U_ j\Subset T_ j\Subset W_ j$, for instance sets of points whose coordinate distance from $\overline{W'_ j}$ is less than suitable small constants. The compact set $\overline{T_ j}\setminus U_ j$ is contained in the open set $W_ j\setminus \overline{W'_ j}$, so by proposition 7.1.3 there is a smooth function $f_ j\colon X\to [0,1]$ with compact support in $W_ j\setminus \overline{W'_ j}$ which equals one on a neighborhood of $\overline{T_ j}\setminus U_ j$; in particular $f_ j=0$ on the neighborhood $X\setminus \operatorname{Supp}f_ j$ of $\overline{W'_ j}$. Fix $\eta >0$ so small that $\eta \mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}f_ j\leq \varepsilon \omega $ for every $j$. Since $(1-\varepsilon )\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\varphi _ j\geq \varepsilon \omega $, the functions

\[ \widehat\varphi _ j=\varphi _ j-\eta f_ j \]

belong to $\mathrm{PSH}(W_ j,(1-\varepsilon )\omega )$. On $W=\bigcup _ jT_ j$ define

\[ \psi (x)=\max \{ \widehat\varphi _ j(x):x\in T_ j\} . \]

The set of indices in this maximum changes with $x$. We show that near $Y$ the maximum is nevertheless taken over a fixed set of indices. Let $p\in Y$, and choose $j$ with $p\in W'_ j$; then $f_ j=0$ near $p$, and $j$ occurs in the maximum near $p$. Let $l$ be an index with $p\in \partial T_ l$. Then $p\in \overline{T_ l}\setminus U_ l$, so $f_ l=1$ near $p$. Since $p\in Y\cap W_ l\cap W_ j$, the inequality (14.45) with $\eta /2$ in place of $\eta $ gives, near $p$,

\[ \widehat\varphi _ l=\varphi _ l-\eta \leq \varphi _ j-\eta /2\leq \widehat\varphi _ j. \]

These are inequalities between functions with values in $[-\infty ,\infty )$, valid also at common poles. As there are finitely many indices, $p$ has a neighborhood $O_ p$ on which the indices $l$ with $p\in \partial T_ l$ never affect the maximum, the indices with $p\notin \overline{T_ l}$ do not occur, and the indices with $p\in T_ l$ occur at every point. Thus, on $O_ p$, the function $\psi $ is the maximum of the $\widehat\varphi _ l$ over the fixed finite set of indices $l$ with $p\in T_ l$.

Let $W_0=\bigcup _{p\in Y}O_ p$. A finite maximum of functions in $\mathrm{PSH}(O_ p,(1-\varepsilon )\omega )$ belongs to this class, by lemma 13.1.4(3). Hence $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(W_0,(1-\varepsilon )\omega )$; moreover $\psi $ is continuous on $W_0\setminus Y$, and $\mathrm e^\psi $, which is locally a finite maximum of the functions $\mathrm e^{\widehat\varphi _ l}=\mathrm e^{\varphi _ l}\mathrm e^{-\eta f_ l}$, is Hölder continuous on $W_0$ by lemma 14.7.10(3). On $Y$ every $\widehat\varphi _ l$ restricts to $\varphi -\eta f_ l\leq \varphi $, while $\widehat\varphi _ j=\varphi $ near $p$ for the index $j$ chosen above. Consequently $\psi |_ Y=\varphi $.

Step 5. The global extension. Choose an open neighborhood $G$ of $Y$ with $\overline G\subset W_0$, for instance a finite union of coordinate balls with closures in $W_0$ covering the compact set $Y$. Its boundary $\partial G$ is compact and disjoint from $Y$, and both $\psi $ and $F$ are finite and continuous near $\partial G$. Choose a constant $M$ with $M>\max _{\partial G}(\psi -F)$ (if $\partial G=\varnothing $, every $M$ will do); since $F+M-\psi $ is continuous on $W_0\setminus Y$ and positive on $\partial G$, it is positive on an open neighborhood $G_1\subset W_0\setminus Y$ of $\partial G$. Set

\[ \widetilde\varphi = \begin{cases} \max \{ \psi ,F+M\} & \text{on }G,\\ F+M& \text{on }X\setminus G. \end{cases} \]

The two expressions agree on $G_1$, so $\widetilde\varphi =F+M$ on the open set $G_1\cup (X\setminus \overline G)$, which contains $X\setminus G$.

The function $F+M$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,(1-4\varepsilon )\omega )\subset \mathrm{PSH}(X,(1-\varepsilon )\omega )$, and $\psi \in \mathrm{PSH}(W_0,(1-\varepsilon )\omega )$. Let $B$ be a coordinate ball whose closure lies in a chart, and $h$ a smooth function on $B$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}h=(1-\varepsilon )\omega $ (lemma 13.1.2). Then $F+M+h\in \mathrm{PSH}(B)$ and $\psi +h\in \mathrm{PSH}(G\cap B)$. Every point $x$ of the boundary of $G\cap B$ in $B$ lies in $\partial G$, and $\varlimsup _{G\ni y\to x}(\psi +h)(y)\leq F(x)+M+h(x)$, because $\psi <F+M$ on $G_1$ and $F+M+h$ is continuous at $x$. By proposition 8.2.5, $\widetilde\varphi +h$ is plurisubharmonic on $B$. Since such balls cover $X$, $\widetilde\varphi $ is quasi-plurisubharmonic and $(1-\varepsilon )\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde\varphi \geq 0$, that is, $\widetilde\varphi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ and $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\widetilde\varphi \geq \varepsilon \omega $. Since $F=-\infty $ on $Y$, we have $\widetilde\varphi |_ Y=\psi |_ Y=\varphi $. The function $\widetilde\varphi $ is continuous on $X\setminus Y$, because $\psi $ and $F$ are continuous on $W_0\setminus Y$ and $X\setminus Y$, respectively. Its exponential equals $\max \{ \mathrm e^\psi ,\mathrm e^ M\mathrm e^ F\} $ on $G$ and $\mathrm e^ M\mathrm e^ F$ on the open set $G_1\cup (X\setminus \overline G)$; these two open sets cover $X$, so $\mathrm e^{\widetilde\varphi }$ is Hölder continuous on $X$ by lemma 14.7.10(3).

Step 6. Analytic singularities. Finally suppose that $\varphi $ has analytic singularities (definition 13.1.5). By proposition 13.1.7, so does the holomorphic pullback $\varphi \circ \pi _ j$, which is not identically $-\infty $, and the function $F$ has analytic singularities by Step 1. By the same proposition, $\max \{ \varphi \circ \pi _ j,F\} $ has analytic singularities; adding the smooth function $AR$ changes the bounded remainder in its local presentations (13.1) by a locally bounded function, so $\max \{ \varphi \circ \pi _ j,F\} +AR$ has analytic singularities. By lemma 14.7.11,

\[ \max \{ \varphi \circ \pi _ j,F\} +AR\leq \varphi _ j\leq \max \{ \varphi \circ \pi _ j,F\} +AR+\log 2. \]

Hence a local presentation (13.1) of $\max \{ \varphi \circ \pi _ j,F\} +AR$ yields one of $\varphi _ j$, with the same holomorphic functions and a bounded remainder changed by at most $\log 2$; so $\varphi _ j$ has analytic singularities. Subtracting the smooth function $\eta f_ j$ changes the remainders by bounded functions, and the locally fixed finite maxima of Step 4 preserve analytic singularities by proposition 13.1.7; so $\psi $ has analytic singularities on $W_0$. On $G$, the function $\widetilde\varphi $ is the maximum of $\psi $ and $F+M$, and on the open set $G_1\cup (X\setminus \overline G)$ it equals $F+M$; both functions have analytic singularities. Therefore $\widetilde\varphi $ has analytic singularities on $X$. $\square$

14.8 Global poles on compact complex manifolds

We end the chapter by dropping the Kähler assumption. Recall that a subset of a complex manifold is pluripolar if it is locally contained in pole sets of plurisubharmonic functions (definition 10.1.1), and that by Josefson's theorem (theorem 10.2.1) a pluripolar subset of $\mathbb C^ n$ lies in the pole set of a single plurisubharmonic function on $\mathbb C^ n$. On a compact connected complex manifold, however, every plurisubharmonic function is constant: the set where it attains its maximum is nonempty and closed, by upper semicontinuity, and open, by the maximum principle (proposition 3.1.4) applied in coordinate balls, where plurisubharmonic functions are subharmonic (theorem 8.2.1). A global pole function must therefore be quasi-plurisubharmonic, and we prove that every pluripolar subset of a compact complex manifold, Kähler or not, lies in the pole set of one quasi-plurisubharmonic function (theorem 14.8.7).

Throughout this section $X$ is a connected compact complex manifold of dimension $n\geq 1$, and $\omega $ is a Hermitian form on $X$ (section 7.5), which need not be closed. Then definition 13.1.3 does not apply to $\omega $, but its formula still makes sense: we write $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ for the set of quasi-plurisubharmonic functions $u$ on $X$ with $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u\geq 0$ in the sense of currents, and call its elements $\omega $-plurisubharmonic; for an open set $D\subset X$ the class $\mathrm{PSH}(D,\omega )$ is defined in the same way. When $\omega $ is Kähler, this is the class of definition 13.1.3. We put $\omega _ u=\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u$. By lemma 13.1.4(4), every quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\Phi $ on $X$ satisfies $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\Phi \geq -C\omega $ for some constant $C$, which we may take positive. Consequently $t\Phi \in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ for $0<t\leq 1/C$.

In local coordinates $\beta $ denotes the Euclidean form (7.4), and for a point $a$ of a chart, $\omega (a)$ denotes the form with constant coefficients equal to those of $\omega $ at $a$. A real $(1,1)$-form with constant coefficients $\gamma =\frac{\mathrm i}2\sum _{j,k}c_{jk}\, \mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k$, where the matrix $(c_{jk})$ is Hermitian, is $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}$ of the real quadratic polynomial $\pi \sum _{j,k}c_{jk}z_ j\bar z_ k$, because $\partial \bar\partial (z_ j\bar z_ k)=\mathrm dz_ j\wedge \mathrm d\bar z_ k$.

Since every plurisubharmonic function on $X$ is constant, only the empty set is globally pluripolar in $X$ in the sense of definition 10.1.1. In this section we therefore call a set $A\subset X$ globally pluripolar if $A\subset \{ \Phi =-\infty \} $ for some quasi-plurisubharmonic function $\Phi $ on $X$. Subsets of globally pluripolar sets are again globally pluripolar.

The proof adapts the capacity argument of section 10.1. For a set $A\subset X$, the regularization of the global extremal function $T_ A$, the upper envelope of the $\omega $-plurisubharmonic functions that are nonpositive on $A$, is bounded unless $A$ is globally pluripolar (lemma 14.8.4). If a piece of a pluripolar set were not globally pluripolar, we would approximate it by compact sublevel sets of small capacity. The Monge–Ampère measure of the extremal function of a compact set is carried by that set, and its mass is controlled by the capacity (proposition 14.8.6), so in the limit we would obtain a bounded potential with zero Monge–Ampère mass. Since $\omega $ need not be closed, Stokes' formula does not compute the total mass $\int _ X(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$; its positivity follows from a comparison principle for small gaps (proposition 14.8.3), in which the error terms produced by $\mathrm d\omega $ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\omega $ are absorbed by the size of the gap. To show that the Monge–Ampère measure of an extremal function vanishes off its set, we freeze the coefficients of $\omega $ on small balls and use the homogeneous balayage of corollary 9.6.4 and a local estimate for nearby bounded potentials (lemma 14.8.5).

We first define products of the currents $\omega _ u$ for bounded $u$. They are built from Bedford–Taylor products (definition 9.1.1), which do not depend on the chart in which they are computed (proposition 9.1.2(2),(5)) and are symmetric in their factors (theorem 9.2.5). We use the following rule for adding smooth functions to the potentials. Let $v_1,\ldots ,v_ k$, $0\leq k\leq n$, be locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions on an open set $\Omega \subset \mathbb C^ n$, and let $g_1,\ldots ,g_ k$ be smooth real functions on $\Omega $ such that the functions $v_ l+g_ l$ are plurisubharmonic. Then

14.48
\begin{equation} \bigwedge _{l=1}^ k\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ l+g_ l)=\sum _{I\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,k\} }\Bigl (\bigwedge _{l\in I}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l\Bigr )\wedge \bigwedge _{l\notin I}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ l, \end{equation}

where the products of the currents $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l$ and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ l+g_ l)$ are Bedford–Taylor products, and the forms $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ l$ are smooth.

We prove (14.48) by induction on $k$; for $k=0$ both sides equal $1$. Let $k\geq 1$ and assume (14.48) for $k-1$ factors, so that $R=\bigwedge _{l\geq 2}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_ l+g_ l)=\sum _ IS_ I\wedge \Gamma _ I$, where $I$ runs over the subsets of $\{ 2,\ldots ,k\} $, $S_ I=\bigwedge _{l\in I}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_ l$, and $\Gamma _ I=\bigwedge _{l\in \{ 2,\ldots ,k\} \setminus I}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ l$ is a smooth closed form of pure bidegree. By definition, $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_1+g_1)\wedge R=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_1R)+\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g_1R)$, and $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(g_1R)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_1\wedge R$ by (9.1). Multiplication of the coefficient measures by $v_1$ commutes with the exterior product by $\Gamma _ I$. Moreover, $\partial \Gamma _ I$ and $\bar\partial \Gamma _ I$ vanish, being the components of two different bidegrees of $\mathrm d\Gamma _ I=0$, so the Leibniz rule for currents (section 7.2) gives $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(R'\wedge \Gamma _ I)=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}R'\wedge \Gamma _ I$ for every current $R'$. Hence $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_1R)=\sum _ I\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(v_1S_ I)\wedge \Gamma _ I=\sum _ I(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}v_1\wedge S_ I)\wedge \Gamma _ I$. Adding the two expansions gives (14.48) for $k$ factors.

Now let $D$ be an open subset of a coordinate chart, identified with an open subset of $\mathbb C^ n$, and let $q$ be a smooth function on $D$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\geq \omega $, for instance a large multiple of $|z|^2$ if $D$ is a ball whose closure lies in the chart. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ is bounded, then $u+q$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+q)=\omega _ u+(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q-\omega )\geq 0$, so it is a locally bounded plurisubharmonic function on $D$ (lemma 13.1.4(2), with $\theta =0$), and $\omega _ u=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+q)+\sigma $ with the smooth form $\sigma =\omega -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q$. For bounded $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, where $1\leq p\leq n$, we define on $D$

14.49
\begin{equation} \omega _{u_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p} =\sum _{J\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,p\} }\Bigl (\bigwedge _{l\in J}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ l+q)\Bigr )\wedge \sigma ^{p-|J|}. \end{equation}

This does not depend on $q$. Let $q'$ be another such function on an open set $D'$. On $D\cap D'$ put $g=q'-q$ and $\gamma =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g$, so that $\sigma '=\omega -\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q'=\sigma -\gamma $. By (14.48), applied to $v_ l=u_ l+q$ and $g_ l=g$, the right side of (14.49) for $q'$ equals

\begin{align*} & \sum _ J\sum _{I\subset J}S_ I\wedge \gamma ^{|J|-|I|}\wedge (\sigma -\gamma )^{p-|J|}\\ & \qquad =\sum _ IS_ I\wedge \sum _{K\subset \{ 1,\ldots ,p\} \setminus I}\gamma ^{|K|}\wedge (\sigma -\gamma )^{p-|I|-|K|} =\sum _ IS_ I\wedge \sigma ^{p-|I|}, \end{align*}

where $S_ I=\bigwedge _{l\in I}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u_ l+q)$ and the last step is the binomial formula for the commuting forms $\gamma $ and $\sigma -\gamma $. Hence the local definitions agree on overlaps, and with a partition of unity subordinate to a cover of $X$ by such sets $D$ (proposition 7.1.3) they define a current of order zero on $X$.

When the $u_ l$ are smooth, proposition 9.1.2(1) shows that this current is the exterior product of the forms $\omega _{u_ l}$. For $u_ p=0$, since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(0+q)\wedge S=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q\wedge S$ for every Bedford–Taylor product $S$ by (9.1), the expansion (14.49) becomes $(\omega _{u_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_{p-1}})\wedge (\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q+\sigma )$; thus a factor $\omega $ in the product is the exterior product with the smooth form $\omega $. The product is symmetric in its factors, because the Bedford–Taylor products are. It is affine in each potential. Indeed, let $u_1,u'_1\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ be bounded and $0\leq t\leq 1$. Then $tu_1+(1-t)u'_1+q=t(u_1+q)+(1-t)(u'_1+q)$, and Bedford–Taylor products are additive and positively homogeneous in each factor (proposition 9.1.2(3)). Hence each term of (14.49) with $1\in J$ is the corresponding combination of the terms for $u_1$ and $u'_1$, while the terms with $1\notin J$ do not involve the first potential, and

\[ \omega _{tu_1+(1-t)u'_1}\wedge \omega _{u_2}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p} =t\, \omega _{u_1}\wedge \omega _{u_2}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p} +(1-t)\, \omega _{u'_1}\wedge \omega _{u_2}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p}. \]

For $u_1=\cdots =u_ p=u$ we write $\omega _ u^ p$, and similarly $\omega _ u^ j\wedge \omega _ v^ k\wedge \omega ^{n-j-k}$ for mixed products. By lemma 14.8.1(2) below, $\omega _ u^ n$ is a positive measure; we call it the Monge–Ampère measure of $u$ with respect to $\omega $. It is the global counterpart of the measure $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u)^ n$ of definition 9.1.1.

Lemma 14.8.1. The class $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ and the products of the currents $\omega _ u$ have the following properties.

  1. A finite maximum of functions in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and so does the upper semicontinuous regularization of the supremum of a nonempty family in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ that is uniformly bounded above. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, $D\subset X$ is open, $v\in \mathrm{PSH}(D,\omega )$, and $\varlimsup _{D\ni y\to x}v(y)\leq u(x)$ for every $x\in \partial D$, then the function equal to $\max \{ u,v\} $ on $D$ and to $u$ on $X\setminus D$ belongs to $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$.

  2. Let $u_1,\ldots ,u_ p\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ be bounded, $1\leq p\leq n$. The current $\omega _{u_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p}$ is positive. The total mass of its trace measure with respect to $\omega $ (section 7.5) is bounded by a constant depending only on $(X,\omega )$ and $\max _ l\sup _ X|u_ l|$, and every pluripolar set has outer measure zero for this trace measure. Moreover, if $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ is bounded and $g$ is a smooth function on an open subset $D$ of a coordinate chart with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\geq \omega $, then $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+g))^ n\geq \omega _ u^ n$ on $D$.

  3. For $1\leq l\leq p$ let $(u_{l,j})_{j\geq 1}$ be sequences in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _{l,j}\sup _ X|u_{l,j}|<\infty $. Suppose that either each sequence decreases pointwise to a function $u_ l$, or each sequence increases and $u_ l$ is the upper semicontinuous regularization of its limit. Then the $u_ l$ are bounded functions in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, and $\omega _{u_{1,j}}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_{p,j}}\to \omega _{u_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p}$ weakly.

  4. Let $L^1(X)$ be taken with respect to the volume form $\omega ^ n$. The set of $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _ Xu=0$ is compact in $L^1(X)$, hence bounded in $L^1(X)$, and $u\mapsto \sup _ Xu$ is continuous on $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ for the $L^1$ topology.

Proof. (1) We freeze the coefficients of $\omega $ on small balls. Fix $\varepsilon >0$, a coordinate chart and a compact subset $L$ of it. By uniform continuity of the coefficients of $\omega $ on a compact neighborhood of $L$ in the chart, there is $r>0$ such that, for every $a\in L$, the ball $B(a,r)$ lies in the chart and the form $\gamma _ a=\omega (a)+\tfrac \varepsilon 2\beta $ satisfies $\omega \leq \gamma _ a\leq \omega +\varepsilon \beta $ on $B(a,r)$. Let $g_ a$ be the real quadratic polynomial with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g_ a=\gamma _ a$ described at the beginning of this section. If $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$, then $u+g_ a$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+g_ a)=\omega _ u+(\gamma _ a-\omega )\geq 0$, so it is plurisubharmonic on $B(a,r)$; in the same way $v+g_ a$ is plurisubharmonic on $D\cap B(a,r)$.

Let $w$ be the function in question: a finite maximum $\max _ iu_ i$, a regularized supremum $(\sup _ iu_ i)^*$, or the pasted function. We show that $w+g_ a$ is plurisubharmonic on $B(a,r)$. In the first case $w+g_ a$ is the maximum of the plurisubharmonic functions $u_ i+g_ a$, which is plurisubharmonic by proposition 8.2.5. In the second case $w+g_ a=(\sup _ i(u_ i+g_ a))^*$, because $g_ a$ is continuous, and it is plurisubharmonic by theorem 8.3.2, since the family $(u_ i+g_ a)$ is uniformly bounded above on $B(a,r)$. In the third case $w+g_ a$ equals $\max \{ u+g_ a,v+g_ a\} $ on $D\cap B(a,r)$ and $u+g_ a$ elsewhere; every boundary point $x$ of $D\cap B(a,r)$ in $B(a,r)$ lies in $\partial D$, and $\varlimsup _{y\to x}(v+g_ a)(y)\leq u(x)+g_ a(x)$ because $g_ a$ is continuous, so proposition 8.2.5 applies on $B(a,r)$. Hence $w$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic on $B(a,r)$ and $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq \omega -\gamma _ a\geq -\varepsilon \beta $ there. The balls $B(a,r)$, $a\in L$, cover a neighborhood of $L$, and positivity of currents is a local property, so $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq -\varepsilon \beta $ on a neighborhood of $L$. Letting $\varepsilon \downarrow 0$ gives $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}w\geq 0$ on the interior of $L$, and since the chart and $L$ were arbitrary, $w\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$.

(2) We first prove positivity on a coordinate ball $B$ whose closure lies in a chart, choosing $q=C|z|^2$ in (14.49). Let $B'\Subset B$ be a smaller concentric ball, let $\rho _ k$ be radial mollifiers whose radii decrease to zero and are smaller than the distance from $B'$ to $\partial B$, and put $u_{l,k}=(u_ l+q)*\rho _ k-q$ on $B'$. Since $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q$ has constant coefficients and $\int \rho _ k=1$, we have $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{l,k}=(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l)*\rho _ k$, where the convolution acts on the coefficients, and $(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l)*\rho _ k\geq 0$ because mollification preserves positivity (section 7.2). By uniform continuity of the coefficients of $\omega $, there are $\tau _ k\to 0$ with $\omega *\rho _ k\leq \omega +\tau _ k\beta $ on $B'$. Hence the smooth $(1,1)$-forms $\omega _{u_{l,k}}+\tau _ k\beta =(\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l)*\rho _ k+(\omega +\tau _ k\beta -\omega *\rho _ k)$ are positive, and so is their exterior product, by proposition 7.2.1. Expanding this product of the forms $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}((u_ l+q)*\rho _ k)+(\sigma +\tau _ k\beta )$, we obtain

\[ \sum _ J\Bigl (\bigwedge _{l\in J}\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}\bigl ((u_ l+q)*\rho _ k\bigr )\Bigr )\wedge (\sigma +\tau _ k\beta )^{p-|J|}. \]

As $k\to \infty $, the smooth plurisubharmonic functions $(u_ l+q)*\rho _ k$ decrease to $u_ l+q$ by proposition 8.2.2(2), so the Bedford–Taylor products converge weakly by theorem 9.2.5; their masses are bounded on compact subsets of $B'$ uniformly in $k$ by theorem 9.1.3, and the coefficients of $(\sigma +\tau _ k\beta )^{p-|J|}$ converge uniformly to those of $\sigma ^{p-|J|}$. Hence each term converges weakly to the corresponding term of (14.49), the product is a weak limit of positive forms on $B'$, and it is positive. Since such balls $B'$ cover $X$, the product is positive on $X$.

Cover $X$ by finitely many such balls $B'$, with $q$ defined on the larger balls $B$. On compact subsets of $B$, theorem 9.1.3 bounds the trace masses of the Bedford–Taylor products in (14.49) by a constant times a product of the numbers $\sup _ B|u_ l+q|$, and theorem 7.2.5 bounds the total variations of their coefficient measures in the same way. Since $\sigma $ and $\omega $ are smooth, the measure $(\omega _{u_1}\wedge \cdots \wedge \omega _{u_ p})\wedge \omega ^{n-p}$ is a sum of the coefficient measures of these Bedford–Taylor products, and of a smooth density for $J=\varnothing $, multiplied by bounded smooth functions. Summing over the finite cover gives the asserted bound for the total mass of the trace measure. By corollary 10.2.2(3), every pluripolar set has outer measure zero for the total variations of the coefficient measures of Bedford–Taylor products of locally bounded plurisubharmonic functions; it is also Lebesgue null (remarks following definition 10.1.1). The trace measure is a positive measure bounded on each $B'$ by a finite sum of such total variations and of a multiple of Lebesgue measure, so every pluripolar set has outer measure zero for it.

Finally, let $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g\geq \omega $ on $D$. We may take $q=g$ in (14.49) on $D$. Put $A=\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+g)$ and $\gamma =\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}g-\omega \geq 0$, so that $\sigma =-\gamma $ and (14.49) reads $\omega _ u^ k=\sum _{i=0}^ k\binom ki A^ i\wedge (-\gamma )^{k-i}$, where $A^ i$ is a Bedford–Taylor power. Using $\binom nk\binom ki=\binom ni\binom {n-i}{k-i}$ and $\sum _{m=0}^{n-i}\binom {n-i}m(-1)^ m=0$ for $i<n$, we obtain

\[ \sum _{k=0}^ n\binom nk\omega _ u^ k\wedge \gamma ^{n-k} =\sum _{i=0}^ n\binom niA^ i\wedge \gamma ^{n-i}\sum _{k=i}^ n\binom {n-i}{k-i}(-1)^{k-i} =A^ n. \]

The currents $\omega _ u^ k$ are positive and the forms $\gamma ^{n-k}$ are strongly positive, so every term on the left is a positive measure, and $(\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}(u+g))^ n=A^ n\geq \omega _ u^ n$.

(3) On a coordinate ball, add a function $q$ as in (14.49). In the decreasing case, $u_ l$ is bounded, the functions $u_{l,j}+q$ are plurisubharmonic and decrease to $u_ l+q$, which is bounded below, so $u_ l+q$ is plurisubharmonic and $u_{l,j}+q\to u_ l+q$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ by theorem 8.3.1. Hence $u_ l$ is quasi-plurisubharmonic, and $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_ l$ is the weak limit of the positive currents $\omega +\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}u_{l,j}$, so $u_ l\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$. The Bedford–Taylor products of the functions $u_{l,j}+q$ converge weakly by theorem 9.2.5. In the increasing case, $u_ l\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ by (1), and $u_ l$ is bounded. The plurisubharmonic functions $u_{l,j}+q$ increase, and $u_ l+q$ is the upper semicontinuous regularization of their limit, because $q$ is continuous; so $u_{l,j}+q\to u_ l+q$ almost everywhere by theorem 8.3.2, and theorem 9.5.1, applied on the ball, gives the weak convergence of the Bedford–Taylor products. In both cases we multiply by the fixed smooth forms $\sigma ^{p-|J|}$ and sum; a partition of unity subordinate to finitely many balls gives weak convergence on $X$.

(4) Choose finitely many coordinate balls $B_ k$, with closures in charts, that cover $X$, concentric balls $B'_ k\Subset B_ k$ that still cover $X$, and smooth functions $q_ k$ on neighborhoods of $\overline{B_ k}$ with $\mathrm{dd}^{\mathrm{c}}q_ k\geq \omega $. For every $u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ the functions $u+q_ k$ are plurisubharmonic on $B_ k$. Two quasi-plurisubharmonic functions that agree almost everywhere on an open set agree everywhere on it, by lemma 13.1.4(1). We may therefore regard $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ as a subset of $L^1(X)$; on compact subsets of a chart, $\omega ^ n$ is bounded above and below by positive multiples of Lebesgue measure.

Continuity of the supremum. Let $u_ j,u\in \mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $u_ j\to u$ in $L^1(X)$; then $u_ j+q_ k\to u+q_ k$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_ k)$ for every $k$. Choose points $x_ j$ with $u_ j(x_ j)=\sup _ Xu_ j$, which exist because $u_ j$ is upper semicontinuous on the compact manifold $X$, and a subsequence along which $\sup _ Xu_ j\to \varlimsup _ j\sup _ Xu_ j$ and $x_ j\to x$. Choose $k$ with $x\in B_ k$ and a real number $c>u(x)$, and a closed ball $K\subset B_ k$ centered at $x$ on which $u<c$. Since $c+q_ k$ is continuous and $u+q_ k<c+q_ k$ on $K$, the second part of theorem 8.3.4 gives $u_ j+q_ k<c+q_ k$ on $K$ for large $j$; hence $u_ j(x_ j)<c$ for large $j$. Letting $c\downarrow u(x)$ we obtain $\varlimsup _ j\sup _ Xu_ j\leq u(x)\leq \sup _ Xu$. Conversely, choose a subsequence along which $\sup _ Xu_ j$ tends to $s=\varliminf _ j\sup _ Xu_ j$ and $u_ j\to u$ almost everywhere. Then $u\leq s$ almost everywhere, so $s>-\infty $ because $u$ is integrable, and $u\leq s$ everywhere by lemma 13.1.4(1), applied to $u$ and the constant $s$. Thus $\sup _ Xu\leq \varliminf _ j\sup _ Xu_ j$, and the supremum is continuous.

Compactness. Let $(u_ j)$ be a sequence in $\mathrm{PSH}(X,\omega )$ with $\sup _ Xu_ j=0$. On each $B_ k$ the plurisubharmonic functions $u_ j+q_ k$ are bounded above by $\sup _{B_ k}q_ k$. Choose $x_ j$ with $u_ j(x_ j)=0$, pass to a subsequence with $x_ j\to x$, and choose $k_0$ with $x\in B'_{k_0}$. On $B_{k_0}$ the functions $u_ j+q_{k_0}$ do not converge locally uniformly to $-\infty $, since for large $j$ their values at the points $x_ j\in \overline{B'_{k_0}}$ are at least $\min _{\overline{B'_{k_0}}}q_{k_0}$. By theorem 8.3.4, a subsequence converges in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_{k_0})$.
